A continuous cusp closing process for negative Kähler-Einstein metrics
Xin Fu, Hans-Joachim Hein, Xumin Jiang

Abstract
. We give an example of a family of smooth complex algebraic surfaces of degree 6 6 in ℂ ​ ℙ 3 \mathbb{CP}^{3} developing an isolated elliptic singularity. We show via a gluing construction that the unique Kähler-Einstein metrics of Ricci curvature − 1 -1 on these sextics develop a complex hyperbolic cusp in the limit, and that near the tip of the forming cusp a Tian-Yau gravitational instanton bubbles off.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Statement of the Main Theorem

Consider the family
\{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Delta}
of degree
6
algebraic surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{3}
given by the projective closures of the affine sextics

\{(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}:(z_{1}^{6}+z_{1}^{3})+(z_{2}^{6}+z_{2}^{3})+(z_{3}^{6}+z_{3}^{3})=\sigma\}.

(1.1)

Then
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
is smooth for
0<|\sigma|\ll 1
and the singular set
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{sing}
is exactly the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Since
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
is a smooth surface of general type for
\sigma\neq 0
, it admits a unique Kähler-Einstein metric
\omega_{KE,\sigma}
of Ricci curvature
-1
by the Aubin-Yau theorem
[
2
,
40
]
. It is also known by
[
4
,
10
,
23
,
33
]
that on
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{reg}
there exists a unique complete Kähler-Einstein metric
\omega_{KE,0}
of Ricci curvature
-1
. By
[
33
]
we have that
\omega_{KE,\sigma}\to\omega_{KE,0}
as
\sigma\to 0
, locally smoothly on
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus{B_{\varepsilon}(0)}
for every fixed
\varepsilon>0
. By
[
10
,
13
]
the end of
(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{reg},\omega_{KE,0})
is asymptotically complex hyperbolic at an optimal rate. This means it is asymptotic to the end of a finite-volume quotient of the complex hyperbolic plane, or in other words, of the unit ball in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
equipped with the Bergman metric. In particular, the end of
(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{reg},\omega_{KE,0})
is asymptotically locally symmetric, but, of course,
(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{reg},\omega_{KE,0})
cannot be a locally symmetric space.

We view the smoothing of the cuspidal Einstein manifold
(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{reg},\omega_{KE,0})
by the family
(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma},\omega_{KE,\sigma})
as a kind of cusp closing process, somewhat analogous to Thurston’s hyperbolic Dehn surgery and its many generalizations
[
1
,
3
,
15
,
21
,
36
]
, but in another way also quite different because here we have a continuous path of metrics on a fixed smooth
4
-manifold. Thus, the picture in our case is actually closer to the usual cuspidal degenerations of hyperbolic Riemann surfaces, which do not exist in higher-dimensional hyperbolic or complex-hyperbolic geometry due to Mostow rigidity. The most interesting difference from the Riemann surface case, and also to some extent from the hyperbolic Dehn surgery situation, is that in our case there is a nontrivial amount of curvature and topology disappearing into the tip of the cusp. Our purpose in this paper is to make this observation precise.

Main Theorem
.

Fix any
R>0
. For
0<|\sigma|\ll 1
sufficiently small relative to
R
, restrict the metric
\omega_{KE,\sigma}
to
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\cap B_{|\sigma|^{1/3}R}(0)
, multiply it by
|{\log|\sigma|}|^{3/2}
and push it forward under the map

\displaystyle(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\sigma^{-\frac{1}{3}}(z_{1}(1+z_{1}^{3})^{\frac{1}{3}},z_{2}(1+z_{2}^{3})^{\frac{1}{3}},z_{3}(1+z_{3}^{3})^{\frac{1}{3}}).

(1.2)

Then as
\sigma\to 0
, the
C^{0}
distance of the resulting Einstein metric on
\{z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}+z_{3}^{3}=1\}\cap B_{R/2}(0)
to the Tian-Yau gravitational instanton is
O_{R,\varepsilon}(|{\log|\sigma|}|^{-1+\varepsilon})
for all
\varepsilon>0
. This is the only bubble, and it accounts for the total loss of
L^{2}
-curvature and Euler characteristic
(108-9)
in the degeneration.

Figure 1.
(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma},\omega_{KE,\sigma})
for
0<|\sigma|\ll 1
. The Main Theorem describes the red part.

The Tian-Yau gravitational instanton is a complete Ricci-flat Kähler, hence hyper-Kähler metric of volume growth
O(r^{4/3})
and curvature decay
O(r^{-2})
on the smooth complex surface
\{z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}+z_{3}^{3}=1\}
. Its construction goes back to
[
38
]
. There exists more than one such metric even modulo automorphism and scaling. The one that appears in our theorem is characterized by being globally
i\partial\overline{\partial}
-exact and by being asymptotic to a specific model Kähler form at infinity. We emphasize that this model form is completely determined. In particular, it does not even depend on an unknown scaling factor.

See Figure
1
for a rough sketch of the geometry and topology of the main regions of
(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma},\omega_{KE,\sigma})
shortly before the limit. The Main Theorem is of course proved by a gluing construction, which also yields estimates in every other region of
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
. In particular, we recover the locally smooth convergence of
\omega_{KE,\sigma}
to
\omega_{KE,0}
over
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{reg}
from
[
33
]
. However, most likely none of our estimates are sharp.

1.2.
Possible generalizations

We expect that all of our work in this paper goes through

\bullet

for any flat family
\{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Delta}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{N}\times\Delta
of surfaces of general type, embedded by a fixed power of their canonical bundles, such that
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
is smooth for
\sigma\neq 0
,

\bullet

all of the singularities of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
are cones over elliptic curves,

\bullet

and the finite group
{\rm Aut}(\mathcal{X}_{0})
acts transitively on the set of singularities.

Under the first two bullet points it is known from
[
29
, Ch 9]
that the cones are of degree at most
9
. Moreover, any smoothing of such a cone is given by a del Pezzo surface of the same degree containing the elliptic curve as an anticanonical divisor. (For degrees
\geqslant 5
this is also proved in
[
29
, Ch 9]
, while for degrees
\leqslant 4
it follows from the realization of these cones as quasi-homogeneous complete intersections in
[
31
, Satz 1.9]
and from the standard deformation theory of complete intersections
[
22
]
.) We have chosen not to state our theorem in this generality because this would have forced us to introduce a lot of extraneous notation and technical arguments. The third bullet point is a much more pressing issue: as an artifact of our method, we are currently unable to deal with multiple independent singularities. Roughly speaking, each singularity contributes a
1
-dimensional obstruction space to the gluing, and we are currently not dealing with these obstructions in a systematic way—we exploit one “accidental” global degree of freedom, which restricts us to the case of a single singularity modulo
{\rm Aut}(\mathcal{X}_{0})
.

In principle, the same kind of geometry also occurs in higher dimensions: every affine cone over a projective Calabi-Yau manifold admits Kähler-Einstein model metrics of cuspidal geometry. However, if the Calabi-Yau is flat, these cones are never smoothable in dimension
3
and higher
[
25
]
; in particular, they never occur as the infinity divisor in a Tian-Yau manifold that could model the smoothing near the tip of the cusp. For non-flat Calabi-Yaus, this smoothing issue disappears but the cuspidal model metrics have unbounded curvature, so it is impossible to prove (as done in
[
10
,
13
]
in the flat case) that the global Kähler-Einstein metric on
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{reg}
is asymptotic to the cusp model. This is a cuspidal version of the well-known orbifold vs. non-orbifold dichotomy for isolated
conical
singularities of Kähler-Einstein metrics. The non-orbifold conical case was solved in
[
17
]
using Donaldson-Sun theory
[
12
]
, bypassing the
C^{2}
estimate in the theory of the complex Monge-Ampère equation. In the cuspidal case one would either need a cuspidal Donaldson-Sun theory, or a very strong
C^{2}
estimate in unbounded curvature.

In a different direction, in dimension
2
, cones over elliptic curves are not the only singularities that can occur on canonically polarized degenerations of smooth surfaces of general type. Such singularities were classified in
[
24
, Thm 4.24]
. Examples include normal crossing singularities (see
[
26
]
for progress in this direction) as well as the cusps of Hilbert modular surfaces
[
23
, pp.54–57]
. The great advantage of the
2
-dimensional case is that the natural cuspidal model metrics do have bounded curvature.

1.3.
Outline of the proof

There is a very extensive literature on gluing constructions in geometric analysis and more specifically in Kähler geometry, which we will not attempt to survey here. Tian-Yau spaces were used as singularity models in
[
5
]
and
[
18
]
but the settings of these two papers are rather different from ours. In fact, our setting is in some sense a
cubic analog
of the classical smoothing of Kähler-Einstein surfaces with nodal singularities via Eguchi-Hanson/Stenzel gravitational instantons. This gluing construction was carried out by Spotti
[
35
]
and independently (in greater generality) by Biquard-Rollin
[
6
]
. The initial idea of our proof, dating back roughly 10 years, was that something similar can perhaps be done in the cubic situation, based on the following observation.

We identify the singularity
\{z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}+z_{3}^{3}=0\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
with the contraction of the zero section of the total space of a negative line bundle
L
over the corresponding elliptic curve
E\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. On
L
we have a unique (up to scaling) Hermitian metric
h
whose curvature form is minus the flat Kähler form
\omega_{E}
representing the class
2\pi c_{1}(L^{\prime})
, where
L^{\prime}\to E
denotes the line bundle dual to
L
. Thus, as a function on the cubic cone,
h=e^{-\varphi}|z|^{2}
, where
|z|
is the standard Euclidean radius and
\varphi
is a
0
-homogeneous function which, viewed as a function
\varphi:E\to\mathbb{R}
, satisfies
i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi=\omega_{FS}|_{E}-\omega_{E}
. Then the asymptotic model of the Tian-Yau metric is
i\partial\overline{\partial}(\log h)^{3/2}
on
\{h>1\}
whereas the asymptotic model of the cusp metric is
-3i\partial\overline{\partial}\log(-{\log h})
on
\{h<1\}
. Define
t:=\log h
. Since the complete Tian-Yau metric, i.e., the relevant gravitational instanton, lives on the smooth surface
\{z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}+z_{3}^{3}=1\}
and since we are interested in the degeneration
\{z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}+z_{3}^{3}=\sigma\}
with
\sigma\to 0
, it is natural to pull back by the map
z\mapsto\sigma^{-1/3}z
and thus replace
t
by
t-T
in the Tian-Yau potential,
T:=(2/3)\log|\sigma|
. Then

\displaystyle\omega_{cusp}=-\frac{3}{t}\omega_{E}+\frac{3}{t^{2}}i\partial t\wedge\overline{\partial}t\;\,(t<0),\;\,\omega_{TY}=\frac{3}{2}(t-T)^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{E}+\frac{3}{4}(t-T)^{-\frac{1}{2}}i\partial t\wedge\overline{\partial}t\;\,(t>T).

(1.3)

Thus, both the tangential and the radial metric coefficients match up at
t=(2/3)T
provided that we also rescale
\omega_{TY}
by a factor of
const\cdot|T|^{-3/2}
. This agreement is surprisingly good. In fact, it lets us write down a pre-glued metric on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
whose Ricci potential has sup norm
O(1)
as
\sigma\to 0
.

However, to enter the gluing regime, it turns out that
O(1)
needs to be improved to
o(1)
. Here a new idea is needed. By solving a Calabi ansatz we show that
\omega_{cusp}
belongs to a
1
-parameter family
\omega_{b}
(
b\in\mathbb{R})
of radial Kähler-Einstein metrics on the cubic cone such that
\omega_{0}=\omega_{cusp}
and
\omega_{b}
undergoes a “geometric transition” at
b=0
. For
b>0
,
\omega_{b}
extends to the total space of
L
with an edge singularity along the zero section of
L
. These edge metrics were introduced in
[
5
]
and
[
13
]
. However, in this paper the case
b<0
is more relevant. For
b<0
,
\omega_{b}
is only defined on a subset
t>T
of the cubic cone,
T\sim-const\cdot|b|^{-1/3}
, and it has a
horn
singularity: as
t\to T
,
\omega_{b}\sim const\cdot|b|^{1/2}\cdot i\partial\overline{\partial}(t-T)^{3/2}
.
This is now a perfect match for the end of the Tian-Yau space, leading to a pre-glued metric whose Ricci potential can be
O(|T|^{-(3/2)+\varepsilon})
for any
\varepsilon>0
if the gluing is done sufficiently close to
t=T
.

Since this decay of the Ricci potential almost matches the scaling factor of the Tian-Yau metric, which is the smallest scale in the construction, it follows from the maximum principle and from Savin’s small perturbation theorem
[
32
]
that the difference between
\omega_{KE,\sigma}
and the pre-glued metric goes to zero everywhere except on the Tian-Yau cap. To get control on the cap we need to develop a weighted Hölder space theory as in
[
6
,
35
]
, but unlike in
[
6
,
35
]
the gluing is obstructed. This is because we are now dealing with three neck regions rather than one: the end of the Tian-Yau space, the cusp of
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{reg}
, and the new neck coming from the Calabi ansatz
\omega_{b}
that interpolates between these two. There is a solution to the linearized PDE on the new neck that approaches
1
on the Tian-Yau side and
0
on the cusp side, and we have been unable to rule out this solution using any choice of weight. We use an ad hoc trick to get around this issue (which however prevents us from dealing with multiple independent singularities): the Ricci potential is only defined modulo a constant, and while changing this constant is the same as adding a constant to the solution of the Monge-Ampère equation, the “Einstein modulo obstructions” metric in the sense of
[
28
]
reacts in a nontrivial way to this change.

,

8
,
37
]
and
[
25
]
, respectively, and an anonymous referee for suggestions that greatly improved our exposition. XF was supported by National Key R&D Program of China 2024YFA1014800
and NSFC No. 12401073. HJH was partially supported by the German Research Foundation (DFG) under Germany’s Excellence Strategy EXC 2044-390685587 “Mathematics Münster: Dynamics-Geometry-Structure” as well as by the CRC 1442 “Geometry: Deformations and Rigidity” of the DFG.

2.
Building blocks of the gluing

2.1.
Degenerations of projective surfaces of general type

In this subsection, we give a family of canonically polarized surfaces
\mathcal{X}
over the disk
\Delta
. Let
\mathcal{X}
be a family of degree
6
surfaces (hence canonically polarized) in
\mathbb{CP}^{3}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}:=\{(Z_{1}^{6}+Z^{3}_{1}Z^{3}_{0})+(Z_{2}^{6}+Z^{3}_{2}Z^{3}_{0})+(Z_{3}^{6}+Z^{3}_{3}Z^{3}_{0})=\sigma Z_{0}^{6}\}.

(2.1)

When
Z_{0}\neq 0
, in the affine coordinates
z_{1}=\frac{Z_{1}}{Z_{0}},z_{2}=\frac{Z_{2}}{Z_{0}},z_{3}=\frac{Z_{3}}{Z_{0}}
,
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
is defined by

(z_{1}^{6}+z_{1}^{3})+(z_{2}^{6}+z^{3}_{2})+(z_{3}^{6}+z_{3}^{3})=\sigma.

(2.2)

It is easy to see that this affine surface is smooth for
0<|\sigma|\ll 1
, that the origin is its unique singular point for
\sigma=0
, and that this singularity is locally analytically isomorphic (via
z_{i}\mapsto z_{i}(1+z_{i}^{3})^{1/3}
for
i=1,2,3
) to the singularity defined by the equation

\displaystyle z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}+z_{3}^{3}=0.

(2.3)

Also, when
Z_{0}=0
,
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
is smooth for every
\sigma
. For instance, by symmetry, let us assume
Z_{1}\neq 0
. Then we may take affine coordinates
w_{1}=\frac{Z_{0}}{Z_{1}},w_{2}=\frac{Z_{2}}{Z_{1}},w_{3}=\frac{Z_{3}}{Z_{1}}
. Then the singular locus of
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
will be the common zeros of the following system of equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}1+w_{1}^{3}+w_{2}^{6}+w_{2}^{3}w_{1}^{3}+w_{3}^{6}+w_{3}^{3}w_{1}^{3}-\sigma w_{1}^{6}=0,\\
3w_{1}^{2}+3w_{2}^{3}w_{1}^{2}+3w_{3}^{3}w_{1}^{2}-6\sigma w_{1}^{5}=0,\\
6w_{2}^{5}+3w_{2}^{2}w_{1}^{3}=0,\\
6w_{3}^{5}+3w_{3}^{2}w_{1}^{3}=0.\end{split}

(2.4)

Now observe that if
w_{1}=0
, then it is easy to see that the above system has no common zeros for all values of
\sigma
. Hence when
Z_{0}=0
,
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
is smooth for all
\sigma
.

Obviously
K_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}\cong\mathcal{O}(2)|_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}
. So let us denote the zero locus of the linear homogeneous polynomial
Z_{0}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
by
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
. Also set
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}:=\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}=\{(z_{1}^{6}+z_{1}^{3})+(z_{2}^{6}+z^{3}_{2})+(z_{3}^{6}+z_{3}^{3})=\sigma\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}.

(2.5)

From what we said it is clear that there is a section
\Omega
of the relative canonical bundle
K_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}/\Delta}
whose restriction to
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}
is a nonvanishing holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{\sigma}
with a zero of order
2
at
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
.

Remark 2.1
.

We remark that up to scaling
\Omega_{\sigma}
is the unique holomorphic volume form on the affine manifold
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}
with zeros or poles along
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
. Indeed, if
\hat{\Omega}_{\sigma}
is any other such form, then
H_{\sigma}:=\Omega_{\sigma}/\hat{\Omega}_{\sigma}
is a nowhere vanishing holomorphic function on
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}
with zeros or poles along
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
. Thus, either
H_{\sigma}
or
1/H_{\sigma}
extends to a holomorphic function on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
and is therefore constant. More generally, in order to conclude
H_{\sigma}=const
it suffices to assume that
\hat{\Omega}_{\sigma}
has at most polynomial growth near
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
.

By equation (
2.2
), we know that near the singularity, the family
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
is locally analytically isomorphic to the following family of Tian-Yau spaces (this terminology will become clear in Section
2.3
)

TY_{\sigma}:=\{z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}+z_{3}^{3}=\sigma\}.

(2.6)

We remark here that
TY_{0}
is obviously the affine cone over a smooth elliptic curve
E\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. Thus, for the specific singularity
z=0
in
TY_{0}
, there is a log resolution of singularities

\pi:\hat{L}\to TY_{0}

(2.7)

with exceptional divisor
E
. Here
L\to E
denotes a negative line bundle,
\hat{L}
denotes the total space of
L
and
\pi
is the contraction of the zero section of
\hat{L}
. We also remark that
TY_{\sigma}
is a family of affine varieties in
\mathbb{C}^{3}\times\mathbb{C}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{3}\times\mathbb{C}
and it can be compactified in
\mathbb{CP}^{3}\times\mathbb{C}
by adding the divisor

E=\{Z_{1}^{3}+Z_{3}^{3}+Z_{3}^{3}=0\}

(2.8)

independently of
\sigma
, where
Z_{i}
are the homogeneous coordinates on
\mathbb{CP}^{3}
.

Next, we fix an identification of
TY_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}
locally near the singularity.

Definition 2.2
.

We define a local analytic isomorphism of the family
\Psi_{\sigma}:\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}\to TY_{\sigma}
as follows:

\Psi_{\sigma}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}):=(z_{1}(1+z_{1}^{3})^{\frac{1}{3}},z_{2}(1+z_{2}^{3})^{\frac{1}{3}},z_{3}(1+z_{3}^{3})^{\frac{1}{3}}).

(2.9)

From the implicit function theorem, it is clear that
\Psi_{\sigma}
is invertible near
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(0,0,0).

We will also need to identify
TY_{0}
with
TY_{1}
in some fixed neighborhood of infinity of the two spaces. Of course, this can only be done diffeomorphically, not biholomorphically.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
9
, Lemma 5.5]
)
.

There exist
R>0
and a smooth function

\nu:\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\overline{B}_{R}\to\mathbb{C}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}(z_{i}+\nu(z)\overline{z}_{i}^{2})^{3}=1

(2.10)

for all
z\in TY_{0}\setminus\overline{B}_{R}
.
Moreover,

\partial^{k}\nu(z)=O(|z|^{-4-k})
as
|z|\to\infty
for all
k\geqslant 0
.

Proof.

Taking complex coordinates
z=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
on
S^{5}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
, define a function
f
by

f:S^{5}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C},\;\,f(z,r,y)=3y\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}|z_{i}|^{4}\right)+3y^{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}|z_{i}|^{2}\overline{z}^{3}_{i}\right)+y^{3}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\overline{z}_{i}^{6}\right)-r^{3},

(2.11)

and fix any point
p=(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in S^{5}
. Since

\displaystyle f(p,0,0)=0,\;\,\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}(p,0,0)=3\sum_{i=1}^{3}|p_{i}|^{4}\neq 0,

(2.12)

the implicit function theorem asserts the existence of a unique
smooth function
g_{p}
, defined in some open neighborhood

U_{p}\times[0,\varepsilon_{p})
of
(p,0),
such that

g_{p}(p,0)=0
and
f(z,r,g_{p}(z,r))=0
.
An obvious covering argument on
S^{5}
then yields a smooth function
g:S^{5}\times[0,\varepsilon)\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying

\displaystyle g(z,0)=0,\quad f(z,r,g(z,r))=0.

(2.13)

We now set
R=\varepsilon^{-1}
and define
\nu:\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\overline{B}_{R}\to\mathbb{C}
by

\displaystyle\nu(z)=\frac{1}{|z|}g\left(\frac{z}{|z|},\frac{1}{|z|}\right).

(2.14)

The fact that
\nu
satisfies
\sum_{i=1}^{3}(z_{i}+\nu(z)\overline{z}_{i}^{2})^{3}=1

for each
z\in TY_{0}\setminus\overline{B}_{R}
is straightforward to verify.

Now we show that
\nu(z)=O(|z|^{-4})
. To see this, observe that
\nu(z)|z|^{4}P(z)=1
on
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\overline{B}_{R}
, where

P(z)=3\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{|z_{i}|^{4}}{|z|^{4}}\right)+3(\nu(z)|z|)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{|z_{i}|^{2}\overline{z}^{3}_{i}}{|z|^{5}}\right)+(\nu(z)|z|)^{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\overline{z}_{i}^{6}}{|z|^{6}}\right).

(2.15)

It then suffices to note that
\nu(z)|z|=g(\frac{z}{|z|},\frac{1}{|z|})\to 0
uniformly as
|z|\to\infty
.

In order to complete the proof of the lemma, we show that
\partial^{k}\nu(z)=O(|z|^{-4-k})
for all
k\geqslant 1
. We only do this for
k=1
. To this end, note that
\nu(z)=1/(|z|^{4}P(z))
. Thus, using the expression of
P(z)
in (
2.15
), it suffices to show that
\partial(\nu(z)|z|)=O(|z|^{-1})
. But this follows from (
2.14
) and from the fact that
g
is a smooth function on
S^{5}\times[0,\varepsilon)
. The lemma is proved.
∎

Now we define a diffeomorphism
\Phi
from
TY_{0}\setminus\overline{B}_{R}
onto its image contained in
TY_{1}
as follows:

\Phi(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}):=(z_{1}+\nu(z)\overline{z}_{1}^{2},z_{2}+\nu(z)\overline{z}_{2}^{2},z_{3}+\nu(z)\overline{z}_{3}^{2}).

(2.16)

With this, we can trivialize the family
TY_{\sigma}
diffeomorphically at infinity.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\Phi
be defined by (
2.16
),
m^{\prime}_{\sigma}:TY_{0}\to TY_{0}
by sending
z
to
\sigma^{-1/3}z
and
m_{\sigma}:TY_{\sigma}\to TY_{1}
by sending
z
to
\sigma^{-1/3}z
. Then we define a family of maps
\Phi_{\sigma}:TY_{0}\setminus B_{|\sigma|^{1/3}R}\to TY_{\sigma}
by

\Phi_{\sigma}:=m^{-1}_{\sigma}\circ\Phi\circ m^{\prime}_{\sigma}.

(2.17)

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
z\in TY_{0}
with
|z|\geqslant|\sigma|^{1/3}R
and also set

\nu_{\sigma}(z):=\sigma^{-1/3}\nu(\sigma^{-1/3}z)
. Then

\Phi_{\sigma}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(z_{1}+\nu_{\sigma}(z)\overline{z}_{1}^{2},z_{2}+\nu_{\sigma}(z)\overline{z}_{2}^{2},z_{3}+\nu_{\sigma}(z)\overline{z}_{3}^{2})

(2.18)

with

\partial^{k}\nu_{\sigma}(z)=O(|\sigma||z|^{-4-k})
as
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\to\infty
for all
k\geqslant 0
.

Proof.

By (
2.17
), for
z\in TY_{0}
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(z_{1}+\sigma^{-\frac{1}{3}}\nu(\sigma^{-\frac{1}{3}}z)\overline{z}_{1}^{2},z_{2}+\sigma^{-\frac{1}{3}}\nu(\sigma^{-\frac{1}{3}}z)\overline{z}_{2}^{2},z_{3}+\sigma^{-\frac{1}{3}}\nu(\sigma^{-\frac{1}{3}}z)\overline{z}_{3}^{2}).

(2.19)

Recall that
\partial^{k}\nu(z)=O(|z|^{-4-k})
as
|z|\to\infty
for all
k\geqslant 0
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\partial^{k}\nu_{\sigma}(z)=\sigma^{-\frac{1+k}{3}}(\partial^{k}\nu)(\sigma^{-\frac{1}{3}}z)=O(|\sigma|^{-\frac{1+k}{3}}(|\sigma|^{-\frac{1}{3}}|z|)^{-4-k})=O(|\sigma||z|^{-4-k})

(2.20)

as
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\to\infty
.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

By Lemma
2.5
,
\Phi_{\sigma}
is well-defined if
|z|\geqslant|\sigma|^{1/3}R
for some sufficiently large but fixed
R\gg 1
. However, the gluing will later be carried out in a region where
|z|
is much larger than this. More precisely, the gluing region is
|z|=|\sigma|^{1/3}e^{\zeta\,\cdot\,|{\log|\sigma|}|^{\alpha}}
for some fixed
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
and for
\zeta\in[1,2]
.

Lastly, we use the above identifications to trivialize the family
\{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Delta}
diffeomorphically away from smaller and smaller neighborhoods of the singularity
(0,0,0)\in\mathcal{X}_{0}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

There exists a
C^{5}
diffeomorphism

G_{\sigma}
from
\mathcal{X}_{0}\setminus\{|z|\leqslant 3|\sigma|^{1/3}R\}
onto its image, which is contained in
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\setminus\{|z|\leqslant 2|\sigma|^{\frac{1}{3}}R\}
and contains
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\setminus\{|z|\leqslant 4|\sigma|^{1/3}R\}
, such that the following hold:

(1)

G_{\sigma}
is smooth away from the hyperplane
\{Z_{0}=0\}
and is the identity on this hyperplane.

(2)

Fix
R_{0}\ll 1
so that
\Psi
is defined on
|z|\leqslant 3R_{0}
. Then
G_{\sigma}=\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{0}
for
|z|\leqslant R_{0}
.

(3)

If we denote the complex structure of
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
by
J_{\sigma}
, then for any
r_{0}>0
and any smooth metric
\omega_{0}
on
\mathcal{X}_{0}\setminus\{|z|<r_{0}\}
we have that

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{0}}(G^{*}_{\sigma}J_{\sigma}-J_{0})|_{\omega_{0}}=O_{r_{0},\omega_{0}}(|\sigma|)\;\,\text{on}\;\,\mathcal{X}_{0}\setminus\{|z|<r_{0}\}

(2.21)

for all
\sigma
sufficiently small depending on
r_{0}
and for all
0\leqslant k\leqslant 4
.

Proof.

By construction,
\mathcal{X}=\{(Z,\sigma)\in\mathbb{CP}^{3}\times\Delta:p(Z)=\sigma Z_{0}^{6}\}
for a certain homogeneous sextic
p
. By (
2.4
), all fibers
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}=\{Z\in\mathbb{CP}^{3}:(Z,\sigma)\in\mathcal{X}\}
intersect the plane
\{Z_{0}=0\}
transversely in the smooth curve
\{Z_{0}=0,Z_{1}^{6}+Z_{2}^{6}+Z_{3}^{6}=0\}
. Write
\mathbb{A}^{3}
to denote the affine chart
\{Z_{0}\neq 0\}
and define

q:\mathbb{A}^{3}\to\mathbb{C},\;\,q(Z):=p(Z)/Z_{0}^{6}.

(2.22)

Let
\nabla q
denote the type
(1,0)
gradient of
q
with respect to the standard Euclidean Kähler metric on
\mathbb{A}^{3}
and let
|\nabla q|
denote the Euclidean length of
\nabla q
. Then the smooth
(1,0)
-vector field

V:=\left(\frac{\nabla q}{|\nabla q|^{2}},1\right)\in T^{1,0}(\mathbb{A}^{3}\times\Delta)

(2.23)

is defined at all points of the submanifold
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}:=(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{0}^{sing})\cap(\mathbb{A}^{3}\times\Delta)
and is tangent to
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
. In fact, the time-
\sigma
flow of
V
maps the fiber
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{\tau}
into the fiber
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{\tau+\sigma}
for every
\tau
.

Claim 2.8
.

V
extends from
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{0}^{sing}
as a
C^{5}
vector field vanishing along the infinity divisor.

Proof of Claim
2.8
.

It suffices to check this in the chart
\{Z_{1}\neq 0\}
. Define affine coordinates
w_{1}=Z_{0}/Z_{1},w_{2}=Z_{2}/Z_{1},w_{3}=Z_{3}/Z_{1}
on this chart. Then
d(1/w_{1}),d(w_{2}/w_{1}),d(w_{3}/w_{1})
are an orthonormal
(1,0)
-coframe with respect to the standard Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{A}^{3}=\{w_{1}\neq 0\}
. The following
(0,1)
-frame (written as vectors with respect to
\partial_{\overline{w}_{1}},\partial_{\overline{w}_{2}},\partial_{\overline{w}_{3}})
is metrically dual to this coframe:

V_{1}:=(-w_{1}^{2},-w_{1}w_{2},-w_{1}w_{3}),\;\,V_{2}:=(0,w_{1},0),\;\,V_{3}:=(0,0,w_{1}).

(2.24)

With
\psi(t):=t^{6}+t^{3}
we then have that
q=\psi(1/w_{1})+\psi(w_{2}/w_{1})+\psi(w_{3}/w_{1})
and so

\overline{\nabla q}=\psi^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{w_{1}}\right)V_{1}+\psi^{\prime}\left(\frac{w_{2}}{w_{1}}\right)V_{2}+\psi^{\prime}\left(\frac{w_{3}}{w_{1}}\right)V_{3}.

(2.25)

It follows that
|w_{1}|^{10}|\nabla q|^{2}
is smooth and
\geqslant 36
at
\{w_{1}=0\}
. Using this fact and the transversality of
\mathcal{X}
to the hyperplane
\{Z_{0}=0\}\times\Delta
, we get that the
T^{1,0}\mathbb{CP}^{3}
component of
V
vanishes to order
6
at the infinity divisor in
\mathcal{X}
. Thus,
V
extends as a
C^{5}
vector field.
∎

We may assume that
C^{-1}\leqslant|V|_{\omega_{0}}\leqslant C
for some positive constant
C
on the set
|z|\geqslant\frac{R_{0}}{2}
. Consider the flow
H_{t}
induced by
V
on
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\setminus\{|z|<\frac{R_{0}}{2}\}
. For
\sigma
sufficiently small,
H_{\sigma}
induces a diffeomorphism from
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}\setminus\{|z|<\frac{3R_{0}}{4}\}
onto its image in
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{\sigma}
. Here we use the fact
that
|V|_{\omega_{0}}
is uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\setminus\{|z|<\frac{R_{0}}{2}\}
, so the incompleteness of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
does not affect that
H_{\sigma}
is a diffeomorphism when
\sigma
is sufficiently small and
|z|\geqslant\frac{3R_{0}}{4}
. It follows that on the strip
S:=\{{R_{0}\leqslant|z|\leqslant 2R_{0}}\}\cap\mathcal{X}_{0}
we have two diffeomorphisms

\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{0}
and
H_{\sigma}
onto some region of
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
. Use the map
\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{0}
to
identify
S
with its image inside
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
. Under this identification,
H_{\sigma}
can be seen as a family of embeddings

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{\sigma}:S^{\prime}\to S

(2.26)

from
S^{\prime}:=\{1.1R_{0}\leqslant|z|\leqslant 1.9R_{0}\}\cap\mathcal{X}
into
S
. Since

\tilde{H}_{\sigma}\to{\rm Id}_{S^{\prime}}

as
\sigma\to 0
,

\tilde{H}_{\sigma}
is smoothly isotopic to the identity map on
S^{\prime}
when
\sigma
is sufficiently small. In particular,
\tilde{H}_{\sigma}
is given by the flow of a time-dependent vector field
\tilde{V}_{\sigma}
defined on

\tilde{H}_{\sigma}(S^{\prime})
. Let
\tau:S\to[0,1]
be a smooth cut-off function given by

\tau(z)=\chi(|z|),

where
\chi
is a smooth increasing function equal to zero for
|z|\leqslant 1.3R_{0}
and equal to 1 for
|z|\geqslant 1.7R_{0}.

Let
\tilde{I}_{\sigma}
be the diffeomorphisms generated by the vector field
\tau\tilde{V}_{\sigma}
for

\sigma
sufficiently small. By construction,
\tilde{I}_{\sigma}
is equal to the identity for
|z|\leqslant 1.2R_{0}
and equal to
\tilde{H}_{\sigma}
for
|z|\geqslant 1.8R_{0}.

Thus, the diffeomorphism

G_{\sigma}:=\begin{cases}\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{0}\circ\tilde{I}_{\sigma}&\text{for}\;\,|z|\leqslant 1.8R_{0},\\
H_{\sigma}&\text{for}\;\,|z|\geqslant 1.8R_{0},\end{cases}

(2.27)

satisfies the desired properties.
∎

2.2.
Singular Kähler-Einstein metrics with hyperbolic cusps

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Delta}
be the family of sextic surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{3}
discussed above. Recall that we have removed a family of hyperplane sections
\mathcal{D}=\{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Delta}
with
[2\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}]=K_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}
for all
\sigma\in\Delta
, thus defining a family of affine surfaces
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}=\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
, and that we have also fixed a family of holomorphic volume forms
\Omega_{\sigma}
(unique up to scaling) on
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}
that vanish to order
2
along the infinity divisors
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
.

It is a well-known fact that the regular part of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
admits a unique complete Kähler-Einstein metric
\omega_{KE,0}
with Ricci curvature
-1
. This fact can nowadays be viewed as a very special case of a general theory of complete Kähler-Einstein metrics on log-canonical models; see
[
4
, Thm A]
and
[
33
, Section 3]
. However, in the
2
-dimensional case—particularly for surfaces of general type with elliptic singularities such as our
\mathcal{X}_{0}
—the required existence result actually goes back to work of R. Kobayashi
[
23
, Thm 1]
and of Cheng-Tian-Yau
[
8
,
37
]
. We now briefly review the construction of
\omega_{KE,0}
in our example.

In our example, recall that
K_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}\cong\mathcal{O}(2)|_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}
. Fix the embedding
\mathbb{CP}^{3}\to\mathbb{CP}^{9}
induced by
\mathcal{O}(2)
as

\displaystyle[Z_{0},Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3}]\longrightarrow[Z_{0}^{2},Z_{0}Z_{1},Z_{0}Z_{2},Z_{0}Z_{3},Z_{1}^{2},Z_{1}Z_{2},Z_{1}Z_{3},Z_{2}^{2},Z_{2}Z_{3},Z_{3}^{2}].

(2.28)

Restricting the Fubini-Study metric on
\mathbb{CP}^{9}
to the image of
\mathbb{CP}^{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\omega_{FS}&:=i\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|Z_{0}^{2}|^{2}+|Z_{0}Z_{1}|^{2}+|Z_{0}Z_{2}|^{2}+|Z_{0}Z_{3}|^{2}+|Z_{1}^{2}|^{2}+|Z_{1}Z_{2}|^{2}+|Z_{1}Z_{3}|^{2}\\
&+|Z_{2}^{2}|^{2}+|Z_{2}Z_{3}|^{2}+|Z_{3}^{2}|^{2}).\end{split}

(2.29)

On the affine piece
Z_{0}\neq 0
, we set

\psi_{FS}:=\log(1+|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}+|z_{3}|^{2}+|z_{1}|^{4}+|z_{1}z_{2}|^{2}+|z_{1}z_{3}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{4}+|z_{2}z_{3}|^{2}+|z_{3}|^{4}).

(2.30)

For better emphasis, we also introduce the notation

\displaystyle\omega_{FS,\sigma}:=\omega_{FS}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}},\;\,\psi_{FS,\sigma}:=\psi_{FS}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}}\;\,\text{for all}\;\,\sigma\in\Delta.

(2.31)

Now let us fix a volume form
V
on
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, which is induced by the Fubini-Study metric of the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(1)
on
\mathbb{CP}^{9}
, such that

\omega_{FS,0}=i\partial\overline{\partial}\log V.

(2.32)

Then the complex Monge-Ampère equation of interest on
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is given by

(\omega_{FS,0}+i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{0})^{2}=e^{\psi_{0}}V.

(2.33)

We have the following existence result from
[
23
, Thm 1]
,
[
4
, Thm C]
,
[
33
, Lemma 3.6]
, together with an asymptotic estimate of the solution
\psi_{0}
from
[
11
, Prop D]
,
[
34
, Prop 3.1]
,
[
10
, Thm 1.1]
.

Theorem 2.9
.

The equation (
2.33
) admits a solution
\psi_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{reg})
satisfying

\displaystyle-(3+\varepsilon)\log(-{\log|\sigma_{E}|_{h_{E}}})-C_{\varepsilon}\leqslant\pi^{*}(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{0}\leqslant C

(2.34)

for every
\varepsilon>0
, where
\pi
is the resolution (
2.7
),
\sigma_{E}
is a defining section of
E
inside the total space
\hat{L}
and
h_{E}
is any Hermitian metric on
L\to E
.

It will be convenient to rewrite the Monge-Ampère equation (
2.33
) in the following manner.

Lemma 2.10
.

Define
\psi_{KE,0}:=\psi_{0}+\psi_{FS,0}
, where
\psi_{0}
is the solution provided by Theorem
2.9
. Then, after adding a constant to
\psi_{KE,0}
, the Kähler-Einstein metric
\omega_{KE,0}:=i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{KE,0}
satisfies

\log\frac{\omega_{KE,0}^{2}}{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}=\psi_{KE,0}.

(2.35)

Proof.

By construction, (
2.33
), we have that

\log\frac{\omega_{KE,0}^{2}}{V}=\psi_{KE,0}-\psi_{FS,0}.

(2.36)

Recall that by definition,
V
is the unique real volume form on the regular part of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
that, viewed as a Hermitian metric on
K_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
, maps to the Fubini-Study metric
h_{FS}
under the adjunction isomorphisms
K_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{CP}^{3}}(2)|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{CP}^{9}}(1)|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
. On the other hand, also by construction,
\Omega_{0}
vanishes to order
2
along the hyperplane section
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\mathcal{X}_{0}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{0}
and vanishes nowhere else. Thus, under the above adjunctions,
\Omega_{0}
maps to some nonzero multiple of the section
S
of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{CP}^{9}}(1)|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
that cuts out the hyperplane
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. The square of the Hermitian norm of
\Omega_{0}
with respect to
V
is
(\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0})/V
, and
|S|_{h_{FS}}^{2}=-\psi_{FS,0}
. Thus, we get (
2.35
) from (
2.36
) by adding
\psi_{FS,0}+const
to both sides.
∎

We now turn to a more precise asymptotic description of
\psi_{KE,0}
near the singularity
p\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
. After introducing the relevant radial model potential
\psi_{cusp}
and proving some technical lemmas, we will give the precise asymptotics of
\psi_{KE,0}
in Proposition
2.14
, which is the final result of this section.

Working on the line bundle
L
whose total space
\hat{L}
resolves the singularity, let
h
denote the Hermitian metric on
L
(unique up to scale) whose curvature form is minus the flat Kähler form
\omega_{E}
representing
2\pi c_{1}(L^{\prime})\in H^{1,1}(E,\mathbb{R})
. Here
L^{\prime}
denotes the dual of
L
. We also denote the positively curved Hermitian metric dual to
h
by
h^{\prime}
. Abusing notation, we view
h
as a function on the total space of
L
via

h:\hat{L}\to\mathbb{R},\;\,\xi\mapsto|\xi|_{h}^{2}.

(2.37)

We consider radial Kähler metrics
\omega=i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi(t)
on
\{0<h\leqslant\delta\}
, where
\psi:(-\infty,\log\delta]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and

\displaystyle t:=\log h.

(2.38)

Since the discussion of these model Kähler metrics (here as well as in all subsequent sections) works more or less the same way in all dimensions
n
with
(L^{\prime},h^{\prime})\to(E,\omega_{E})
a polarized compact Calabi-Yau
(n-1)
-fold, we prefer to
not
specialize our computations of these metrics to the case
n=2
.

Continuing to work in the general
n
-dimensional setting, we fix a nowhere vanishing holomorphic
(n-1)
-form
\Omega_{E}
on
E
with

\omega_{E}^{n-1}=i^{(n-1)^{2}}\Omega_{E}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{E}.

(2.39)

Note that
\Omega_{E}
is unique up to multiplication by a unit complex number. Using this, we now construct a holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
on the total space of the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-bundle associated with
L
or
L^{\prime}
, i.e., on the complement of the zero section in the total space of either one of these two line bundles. This form
\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
will be unique up to sign, and will have first-order poles along both zero sections such that its residues at these poles are equal to
\pm\Omega_{E}
. Precisely, let
p
denote the bundle projection from the total space of the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-bundle onto
E
and let
\pm Z
denote the
(1,0)
-vector field on the total space that generates the natural
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action coming from either
L
or
L^{\prime}
. Then
\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
is determined by the equation

\pm Z\lrcorner\ \Omega_{\mathcal{C}}=p^{*}\Omega_{E}.

(2.40)

A radial
(1,1)
-form
\omega=i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi(t)
as above is positive definite if and only if

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(t),\psi^{\prime\prime}(t)>0.

(2.41)

Then
\omega
is a Kähler-Einstein metric if (
2.41
) holds and

\displaystyle(\psi^{\prime})^{n-1}\psi^{\prime\prime}=e^{\psi+a}

(2.42)

for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
\psi
satisfies
(
2.42
), then given any
k\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the function
\Psi(t):=\psi(kt)
satisfies

\displaystyle(\Psi^{\prime})^{n-1}\Psi^{\prime\prime}=e^{\Psi+a+(n+1)\log k},

(2.43)

which is the same as equation (
2.42
) except having a different
a
. The solutions related to the cuspidal model Kähler-Einstein metrics in our previous paper
[
13
]
are

\displaystyle\psi_{cusp}(t)=-(n+1)\log(-t),\;\,e^{a}=(n+1)^{n}.

(2.44)

Given an arbitrary
a
, the model solution is defined as

\displaystyle\psi_{cusp}(t):=-(n+1)\log(-t)+n\log(n+1)-a,\;\,\omega_{cusp}:=i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{cusp}.

(2.45)

Lemma 2.11
.

Adding a constant to
\psi_{cusp}
, i.e., choosing the constant
a
in (
2.45
) correctly, one has

e^{-\psi_{cusp}}\omega_{cusp}^{n}=i^{n^{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}.

(2.46)

Proof.

It follows from the symmetry of
\omega_{cusp},\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
and from the Kähler-Einstein condition that

\displaystyle g(t):=\frac{e^{-\psi_{cusp}}\omega_{cusp}^{n}}{i^{n^{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}}

(2.47)

is a pluriharmonic function depending only on
t
. Now

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}g(t)=g^{\prime}(t)i\partial\overline{\partial}t+ig^{\prime\prime}(t)\partial t\wedge\overline{\partial}t=0.

(2.48)

So
g(t)
must be a constant and (
2.46
) can be achieved by adding a constant to
\psi_{cusp}
.
∎

To compare
\psi_{KE,0}
to
\psi_{cusp}
in our main example, it is helpful to rescale the holomorphic volume forms
\Omega_{\sigma}
by a constant so that
\Omega_{0}
becomes asymptotic to
\Psi_{0}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
at the singularity
p\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
.

Lemma 2.12
.

For fixed
\Omega_{0}
and
\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
, there is a nonzero complex constant
C
such that

(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}(\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0})=|C|^{2}e^{-v}{\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}},

(2.49)

where
v
is a pluriharmonic function satisfying for all
\varepsilon>0
and
j\geqslant 1
and for
t\to-\infty
that

|v|=O(e^{\frac{t}{2}}),\;\,|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{j}v|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O_{\varepsilon,j}(e^{\frac{(1-\varepsilon)t}{2}}).

(2.50)

Proof.

The function
H:=[(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\Omega_{0}]/\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
is a nowhere vanishing holomorphic function on
U\setminus\{0\}
for some small open neighborhood
U
of the singularity
0\in TY_{0}
. Since this is a normal isolated surface singularity in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, the Hartogs principle
[
30
, Thm 1.1]
says that
H
extends to a holomorphic function on
U
. We must have that
H(0)\neq 0
because otherwise
\lim_{z\to 0}1/H(z)=\infty
, contradicting the Hartogs principle for
1/H
. Let
C:=H(0)
. Then (
2.49
) holds with
v(z):=\log|H(0)/H(z)|^{2}
, which is obviously pluriharmonic and obviously satisfies
|v(z)|=O(|z|)=O(e^{t/2})
as
|z|\to 0
resp.
t\to-\infty
.

Now we further estimate
|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{j}v|_{\omega_{cusp}}
for all
j\geqslant 1
. Since
v
is in particular harmonic with respect to
\omega_{cusp}
and since
\omega_{cusp}
is a hyperbolic metric for
n=2
, these derivatives can be estimated using Schauder theory on the universal covers of
\omega_{cusp}
-geodesic balls of some fixed radius
r_{0}\in(0,1]
. Coordinates on these local universal covers that make
\omega_{cusp}
uniformly smoothly bounded can be found e.g. in
[
13
, proof of Lemma 3.5]
. The
\varepsilon
-loss in (
2.50
) is due to the fact that the function
t
(which coincides with
-1/x
in the notation of
[
13
]
) varies by a factor of
1+O(r_{0})
over any
\omega_{cusp}
-ball of radius
r_{0}\ll 1
.
∎

By a common rescaling of the forms
\Omega_{\sigma}
, which was our only freedom in choosing these forms, we can now arrange that
C=1
in (
2.49
), i.e., that
\Omega_{0}
is asymptotic to
\Psi_{0}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
near the singularity
p\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
. According to Lemma
2.10
, this leads to an additive normalization of
\psi_{KE,0}
such that (
2.35
) holds, and this equation then matches the equation (
2.46
) satisfied by
\psi_{cusp}
to leading order at the singularity.

We require one additional technical lemma before stating our final result.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
U
be a neighborhood of the singularity
p=(0,0,0)
in
TY_{0}
. Let
\varphi
be a function on
U\setminus\{p\}
satisfying
i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi=0
and
|\varphi|=o(-{\log|z|})=o(-t)
as
|z|\to 0
resp.
t\to-\infty
. Then
\varphi
extends continuously as a PSH function on
U
. Moreover,
\varphi
is the real part of a holomorphic function near the origin of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, so, after subtracting a constant,
|\varphi|=O(|z|)=O(e^{t/2})
as
|z|\to 0
resp.
t\to-\infty
.

Proof.

Consider the resolution of singularities
\pi:\hat{L}\to TY_{0}
from (
2.7
). The exceptional divisor is an elliptic curve
E
and
\hat{L}
is the total space of a negative holomorphic line bundle
L
over
E
. For any point
q\in E
, there is a local holomorphic trivialization of the line bundle
\hat{L}|_{V}\cong V\times\mathbb{C}
for some neighborhood
V\ni q
. Let
w_{1}
be the coordinate of
V
and
w_{2}
be the coordinate of
\mathbb{C}
. Then by assumption,
\varphi
restricted to any fiber of
L
is a smooth harmonic function on the punctured disk
\Delta^{*}
with a sub-logarithmic pole at
0
. Hence for each fixed
w_{1}
,
\varphi
can be extended continuously across
0
. So for fixed
w_{1}
,
\varphi
is the real part of a holomorphic function on
\Delta
. By the mean value theorem,

\varphi(w_{1},0)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\gamma}\varphi(w_{1},w_{2})\,dw_{2},

(2.51)

where
\gamma
is a fixed circle centered at
0
. Taking
i\partial\overline{\partial}
with respect to the variable
w_{1}
, we have that
\varphi(w_{1},0)
is a harmonic function of
w_{1}
. Notice that
E
is compact, so
\varphi(w_{1},0)
is a constant. Hence
\varphi
can be extended continuously across
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{sing}
as a PSH function on
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.

Now we show that
\varphi
is the real part of a holomorphic function. Note that because the line bundle
\hat{L}
is homotopy equivalent to its zero section
E
, the de Rham cohomology
H^{1}(\hat{L},\mathbb{R})
is naturally isomorphic to
H^{1}(E,\mathbb{R})
. Since
i\partial\overline{\partial}\pi^{*}\varphi=0
,
d^{c}\pi^{*}\varphi
is a closed real
1
-form. So we have

\displaystyle d^{c}\pi^{*}\varphi=dg+a\alpha+b\beta,

(2.52)

where
a,b
are constants,
g
is a function on an open neighborhood of the zero section of
\hat{L}
and
\alpha,\beta
are closed
1
-forms on
E
generating
H^{1}(E,\mathbb{R})
. Let
\gamma_{\alpha},\gamma_{\beta}
be loops in
E
whose homology classes are Poincaré dual to
\alpha,\beta
. Note that
\varphi
is constant on
E
and
dg
is exact, so the integrals of the
1
-form
d^{c}\pi^{*}\varphi-dg
along the loops
\gamma_{\alpha},\gamma_{\beta}
are zero. So
a,b
are zero. So
f:=\pi^{*}\varphi-ig
is a holomorphic function with real part
\pi^{*}\varphi
, necessarily constant along the zero section
E
. After pushing down to the singularity, we can locally extend
f
to a holomorphic function on an open set of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. By subtracting a constant, we may assume that
f(p)=0
. By Taylor expansion, we deduce that
f=O(|z|)
.
∎

We are now able to prove the following precise asymptotic comparison of
\psi_{KE,0}
and
\psi_{cusp}
. Modulo the above lemmas this is the statement of
[
13
, Main Theorem]
, whose proof relies on an earlier partial result from
[
10
, Thm 1.4]
. The results of
[
10
,
13
]
can be generalized to all dimensions
n\geqslant 2
assuming that the polarized Calabi-Yau
(n-1)
-fold
(L^{\prime},h^{\prime})\to(E,\omega_{E})
in the above discussion of
\psi_{cusp}
is flat.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
\Psi_{\sigma}
be the local holomorphic isomorphism defined in equation (
2.9
). Then

\displaystyle(\Psi^{-1}_{0})^{*}\psi_{KE,0}-\psi_{cusp}=u+v,

(2.53)

where for some constants
\mathfrak{s}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\delta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, for all
j\geqslant 0
and for
t\to-\infty
,

\left|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{j}\left(u+3\log\left(1-\frac{\mathfrak{s}}{t}\right)\right)\right|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O_{j}(e^{-\delta_{0}\sqrt{-t}}),

(2.54)

and where
v
is a pluriharmonic function satisfying for all
\varepsilon>0
and
j\geqslant 1
and for
t\to-\infty
that

|v|=O(e^{\frac{t}{2}}),\;\,|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{j}v|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O_{\varepsilon,j}(e^{\frac{(1-\varepsilon)t}{2}}).

(2.55)

Remark 2.15
.

Composing both sides of (
2.53
) with the automorphism
scale_{\lambda}(z)=\lambda z
of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
yields a new decomposition

(\Psi_{0}^{-1}\circ scale_{\lambda})^{*}\psi_{KE,0}-\psi_{cusp}=u_{\lambda}+v_{\lambda}
,
where
u_{\lambda},v_{\lambda}
satisfy the same properties as before except that the constant
\mathfrak{s}
in (
2.54
) gets replaced by
\mathfrak{s}-\log\lambda
. Thus, by choosing
\lambda=e^{\mathfrak{s}}
we obtain that
u_{\lambda}
is purely exponentially decaying and contains no powers of
1/t
in its expansion as
t\to-\infty
. This was already pointed out in the introduction to our previous paper
[
13
]
.

This suggests making the following modification to our setup: For
\lambda=e^{\mathfrak{s}}
, replace
\Psi_{\sigma}:\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}\to TY_{\sigma}
by
{scale}_{\lambda^{-1}}\circ\Psi_{\sigma}:\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}\to TY_{\lambda^{-3}\sigma}
for all
\sigma\in\Delta
. (The domains of these maps are actually just small open neighborhoods of the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
intersected with
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}
.) In this way, we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathfrak{s}=0
in (
2.54
), i.e., that
u
is exponentially decaying. Note that, so far, we have never actually used the map
\Psi_{\sigma}
with
\sigma\neq 0
except in Lemma
2.7
. The statement and proof of that lemma remain unchanged if we also replace
\Phi_{\sigma}
by
\Phi_{\lambda^{-3}\sigma}
, which again takes values in
TY_{\lambda^{-3}\sigma}
.

However,
\Psi_{\sigma},\Phi_{\sigma}
will also be used as gluing maps in Section
3
. The replacements
\Psi_{\sigma}\leadsto{scale}_{\lambda^{-1}}\circ\Psi_{\sigma}
and
\Phi_{\sigma}\leadsto\Phi_{\lambda^{-3}\sigma}
do not worsen any of the estimates in Section
3
(or later), but being able to assume that
\mathfrak{s}=0
in (
2.54
) drastically reduces the gluing error. To exploit this improvement without having to introduce even more notation, we will from now on simply assume that
\mathfrak{s}=0
and
\lambda=1
.

Proof of Proposition
2.14
.

First of all, if we define

\displaystyle u:=\log\left(\frac{(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\omega^{2}_{KE,0}}{\omega^{2}_{cusp}}\right),

(2.56)

then by the Kähler-Einstein equation, we have that

\displaystyle(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\omega_{KE,0}-\omega_{cusp}=i\partial\overline{\partial}u.

(2.57)

By
[
13
, Main Theorem]
, there exist
\mathfrak{s}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\delta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for all
j\geqslant 0
and for
t\to-\infty
,

\displaystyle\left|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{j}\left(u+3\log\left(1-\frac{\mathfrak{s}}{t}\right)\right)\right|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O_{j}(e^{-\delta_{0}\sqrt{-t}}).

(2.58)

Here we remark again that the function
-1/t
in this paper is the same as the function
x
used in
[
13
]
.
So at the Kähler potential level, we have that

(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{KE,0}-\psi_{cusp}=\varphi+u,

(2.59)

where
\varphi
is a pluriharmonic function. By the estimate (
2.34
) of
\psi_{KE,0}
near the singularity and by the definition of
\psi_{cusp}
, we have
|\varphi|=O(\log(-t))=o(-t)
. Thus, by Lemma
2.13
,
\varphi=\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{v},
where
\mathbf{c}
is a constant and
\mathbf{v}
is a pluriharmonic function satisfying
|\mathbf{v}|=O(|z|)=O(e^{t/2})
.

It is now easy to see that the constant
\mathbf{c}
is zero. Indeed,

e^{-\psi_{cusp}}\omega_{cusp}^{2}=\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}=e^{v}(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}(\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0})=e^{v}(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}(e^{-\psi_{KE,0}}\omega^{2}_{KE,0})

(2.60)

by (
2.46
), by (
2.49
) with our chosen normalization
C=1
, and by (
2.35
). Therefore,

{\mathbf{c}+u+\mathbf{v}}=(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{KE,0}-\psi_{cusp}=\log\left(\frac{(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\omega^{2}_{KE,0}}{\omega^{2}_{cusp}}\right)+v=u+O(e^{\frac{t}{2}}).

(2.61)

Since
\mathbf{v}
goes to zero at the singularity, we get
\mathbf{c}=0
. Moreover,
\mathbf{v}=v
, so (
2.50
) implies (
2.55
).
∎

2.3.
Tian-Yau metrics

In this subsection we review the Tian-Yau construction
[
38
, Thm 4.2]
of a complete Ricci-flat Kähler metric on the complement of a smooth anti-canonical divisor in a smooth Fano manifold. Expositions of this construction can be found in
[
18
, Section 3]
and
[
19
, Section 3]
. We will borrow freely from these two references and show how to match their notation to ours.

As in our discussion of cusp metrics above, let
E
be an
(n-1)
-dimensional compact Kähler manifold
with trivial canonical bundle
K_{E}
and let
L^{\prime}\to E
be an ample line bundle, the dual of a negative line bundle
L\to E
. We fix a nowhere vanishing
holomorphic
(n-1)
-form
\Omega_{E}
on
E
with

\int_{E}i^{(n-1)^{2}}\Omega_{E}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{E}=(2\pi c_{1}(L^{\prime}))^{n-1}.

(2.62)

By Yau’s resolution of the Calabi conjecture
[
40
]
, there exists a unique Ricci-flat Kähler metric
\omega_{E}
on
E
representing the Kähler class
2\pi c_{1}(L^{\prime})\in H^{1,1}(E,\mathbb{R})
and satisfying the equation

\omega_{E}^{n-1}=i^{(n-1)^{2}}\Omega_{E}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{E}.

(2.63)

Up to scaling, there exists a unique Hermitian metric
h^{\prime}
on
L^{\prime}
whose curvature form is
\omega_{E}
, and
h^{\prime}
is the dual of a negatively curved Hermitian metric
h
on
L
. We
now fix a choice of
h^{\prime}
. Then the
Calabi model space
is the subset
\mathcal{C}
of the total space of
L^{\prime}
consisting
of all elements
\xi
with
0<|\xi|_{h^{\prime}}<1
, endowed with a nowhere vanishing holomorphic volume form

\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
and a Ricci-flat Kähler metric
\omega_{\mathcal{C}}
which is incomplete as
|\xi|_{h^{\prime}}\to 1
and complete as
|\xi|_{h^{\prime}}\to 0.

Again as in our discussion of cusp metrics above, the holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
is uniquely determined by the equation

Z\lrcorner\ \Omega_{\mathcal{C}}=p^{*}\Omega_{E},

(2.64)

where
p:\mathcal{C}\to E
is the bundle projection and
Z
is the holomorphic vector field generating the natural
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on the fibers of
p
(i.e., the one coming from the line bundle structure of
L^{\prime}
). The metric
\omega_{\mathcal{C}}
is given by the Calabi ansatz

\omega_{\mathcal{C}}:=i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{\mathcal{C}},\;\,\psi_{\mathcal{C}}:=\frac{n}{n+1}(-{\log|\xi|_{h^{\prime}}^{2}})^{\frac{n+1}{n}},

(2.65)

and satisfies the Monge-Ampère equation

\omega_{\mathcal{C}}^{n}=i^{n^{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}},

(2.66)

hence is Ricci-flat. Also define the momentum coordinate

\mathbf{z}:=(-{\log|\xi|^{2}_{h^{\prime}}})^{\frac{1}{n}}.

(2.67)

Then the
\omega_{\mathcal{C}}
-distance to a fixed point in
\mathcal{C}
is uniformly comparable to
\mathbf{z}^{(n+1)/2}
for
z\gg 1
.

We now explain the Tian-Yau construction
[
38
]
of complete Ricci-flat Kähler metrics asymptotic to a Calabi ansatz at infinity. Let
M
be a smooth Fano manifold of complex dimension
n
, let
E\in|K_{M}^{-1}|
be a smooth divisor, and let
L^{\prime}
denote the holomorphic normal bundle to
E
in
M
. Then
E
has a trivial canonical bundle and
L^{\prime}
is ample, so in particular we can choose a holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{E}
on
E
which satisfies (
2.62
). We fix a defining section
S
of
E
, so that
S^{-1}
can be viewed as a holomorphic
n
-form
\Omega_{X}
on
X=M\setminus E
with a simple pole along
E
. After scaling
S
by a nonzero complex constant, we may assume that
\Omega_{E}
is the residue of
\Omega_{X}
along
E
. (In practice this means that
\Omega_{X}
is asymptotic to
\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
with respect to a suitable diffeomorphism
\Phi
between tubular neighborhoods of
E
in
M
and of the zero section in
L^{\prime}
.) Lastly, we fix a Hermitian metric
h_{M}
on
K_{M}^{-1}
whose curvature form is strictly positive on
M
and restricts to the unique Ricci-flat Kähler form
\omega_{E}\in 2\pi c_{1}(L^{\prime})
on
E
. Then

\omega_{X}^{\circ}:=\frac{n}{n+1}i\partial\overline{\partial}(-{\log|S|_{h_{M}}^{2}})^{\frac{n+1}{n}}

(2.68)

defines a Kähler form on a neighborhood of infinity in
X
. This
\omega_{X}^{\circ}
is then complete towards
E
and is asymptotic to
\omega_{\mathcal{C}}
, where the Hermitian metric
h^{\prime}
used in (
2.65
) is the restriction of
h_{M}
to
K_{M}^{-1}|_{E}=L^{\prime}
. In particular,
\omega_{X}^{\circ}
is asymptotically Ricci-flat. Moreover, since
X
is an affine variety, it is reasonably straightforward to extend
\omega_{X}^{\circ}
as a globally defined
i\partial\overline{\partial}
-exact Kähler form on the whole manifold
X
. Then the following is proved in
[
38
]
by solving a Monge-Ampère equation with reference metric
\omega_{X}^{\circ}
. The exponential decay statement follows from
[
16
, Prop 2.9]
.

Theorem 2.16
.

There is a complete Ricci-flat Kähler metric
\omega_{X}
on
X
solving the equation

\omega_{X}^{n}=i^{n^{2}}\Omega_{X}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{X}.

(2.69)

Moreover, there is a unique choice of the scaling factor of
h^{\prime}
resp. of
h_{M}
such that
\omega_{X}=\omega_{X}^{\circ}+i\partial\overline{\partial}\phi
, where for some
\delta_{0}>0
and for all
k\geqslant 0
, as
z\to\infty
,

|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathcal{C}}}^{k}(\phi\circ\Phi)|_{\omega_{\mathcal{C}}}=O_{k}(e^{-\delta_{0}\mathbf{z}^{n/2}}).

(2.70)

Here we have implicitly fixed a smooth identification
\Phi
of
M
and of the total space of
L^{\prime}
near
E
.

We will now apply this construction to our space
X:=TY_{1}=\{z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}+z_{3}^{3}=1\}
, a smooth cubic in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. We can compactify
X
to
M
, a smooth cubic in
\mathbb{CP}^{3}
, by adding an elliptic curve
E
at infinity. Note that
M
is an anticanonically embedded del Pezzo surface and
L^{\prime}=-K_{M}^{-1}|_{E}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{CP}^{3}}(1)|_{E}
. The total space of the dual bundle
L
resolves the singularity of the cubic cone
TY_{0}
at the origin, and
\mathcal{C}
is identified with a neighborhood of infinity in
TY_{0}
. Then our diffeomorphism
\Phi=\Phi_{1}
from (
2.16
), (
2.17
) plays the role of the smooth identification
\Phi
in Theorem
2.16
, and
\mathbf{z}^{2}=t
for
t>0
.

The following lemma records some more detailed estimates from
[
18
, Prop 3.4]
in our setting.

Lemma 2.17
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
and
k\geqslant 0
and for
t\to+\infty
,

|\nabla_{g_{\mathcal{C}}}^{k}(\Phi_{1}^{*}J_{TY_{1}}-J_{\mathcal{C}})|_{g_{\mathcal{C}}}+|\nabla_{g_{\mathcal{C}}}^{k}(\Phi_{1}^{*}\Omega_{TY_{1}}-\Omega_{\mathcal{C}})|_{g_{\mathcal{C}}}=O_{\varepsilon,k}(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)t}).

(2.71)

Moreover, there exists a global potential
\psi_{TY_{1}}
of
\omega_{TY_{1}}
, i.e.,
\omega_{TY_{1}}=i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{TY_{1}}
globally on
TY_{1}
, such that there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that for all
k\geqslant 0
and for
t\to+\infty
,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{g_{\mathcal{C}}}^{k}(\Phi_{1}^{*}\psi_{TY_{1}}-\psi_{\mathcal{C}})|_{g_{\mathcal{C}}}+|\nabla_{g_{\mathcal{C}}}^{k}(\Phi_{1}^{*}\omega_{TY_{1}}-\omega_{\mathcal{C}})|_{g_{\mathcal{C}}}=O_{k}(e^{-\delta_{0}\sqrt{t}}).

(2.72)

In our gluing construction, we will be working with a scaled copy of
\omega_{TY_{1}}
, pulled back from
TY_{1}
to
TY_{\sigma}
via the maps of Definition
2.4
. Let us first recall these maps for convenience:

\begin{split}\Phi:TY_{0}\setminus\overline{B}_{R}\to TY_{1},\;\,\Phi(z)=z+O(|z|^{-2})\;\,\text{as}\;\;|z|\to\infty,\\
m_{\sigma}^{\prime}:TY_{0}\to TY_{0},\;\,m_{\sigma}^{\prime}(z)=\sigma^{-1/3}z,\\
m_{\sigma}:TY_{\sigma}\to TY_{1},\;\,m_{\sigma}(z)=\sigma^{-1/3}z,\\
\Phi_{\sigma}:TY_{0}\setminus\overline{B}_{|\sigma|^{1/3}R}\to TY_{\sigma},\;\,\Phi_{\sigma}=m^{-1}_{\sigma}\circ\Phi\circ m^{\prime}_{\sigma}.\end{split}

(2.73)

Next, we introduce the following pullback objects:

\begin{split}\Omega_{TY_{\sigma}}:=m_{\sigma}^{*}\Omega_{TY_{1}},\;\,\omega_{TY_{\sigma}}:=m_{\sigma}^{*}\omega_{TY_{1}},\;\,\psi_{TY_{\sigma}}:=m_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{TY_{1}}\;\,\text{and}\;\,g_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}:=(m^{\prime}_{\sigma})^{*}g_{\mathcal{C}}.\end{split}

(2.74)

Then Lemma
2.17
implies the following estimates, including an additional rescaling. The strange form of the scaling factor,
|b|^{1/2}
with
b<0
, is due to some conventions in Section
2.4
.

Lemma 2.18
.

For all
b<0
and
\sigma\in\Delta^{*}
the following estimates hold with all of the implied constants independent of
b
and
\sigma
. First, for all
\varepsilon>0
and
k\geqslant 0
and for
t-\frac{2}{3}\log|\sigma|\to+\infty
,

\begin{split}|\nabla_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}^{k}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}J_{TY_{\sigma}}-J_{\mathcal{C}})|_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}&=O_{\varepsilon,k}(|b|^{-\frac{k}{4}}e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-\frac{2}{3}\log|\sigma|)}),\\
|\nabla_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}^{k}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\Omega_{TY_{\sigma}}-\Omega_{\mathcal{C}})|_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}&=O_{\varepsilon,k}(|b|^{-\frac{k}{4}}e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-\frac{2}{3}\log|\sigma|)}).\end{split}

(2.75)

Moreover, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that for all
k\geqslant 0
and for
t-\frac{2}{3}\log|\sigma|\to+\infty
,

\begin{split}|\nabla_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}^{k}(|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{TY_{\sigma}}-|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi_{\mathcal{C},\sigma})|_{{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}}&=O_{k}(|b|^{\frac{2-k}{4}}e^{-{\delta}_{0}(t-\frac{2}{3}\log|\sigma|)^{1/2}}),\\
|\nabla_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}^{k}(|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\omega_{TY_{\sigma}}-|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma})|_{{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}}&=O_{k}(|b|^{-\frac{k}{4}}e^{-{\delta}_{0}(t-\frac{2}{3}\log|\sigma|)^{1/2}}).\end{split}

(2.76)

Proof.

This is trivial except for the following two observations. First,
t-\frac{2}{3}\log|\sigma|=(m_{\sigma}^{\prime})^{*}t
. Second,
J_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}:=(m_{\sigma}^{\prime})^{*}J_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\Omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}:=(m_{\sigma}^{\prime})^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
are actually equal to
J_{\mathcal{C}}
resp.
\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
.
∎

Lastly, we need to compare the holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{TY_{\sigma}}
on
TY_{\sigma}
to the holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{\sigma}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
on a uniform neighborhood of the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Recall the local biholomorphism
\Psi_{\sigma}:\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}\to TY_{\sigma}
from (
2.9
). Then for all
\sigma\in\Delta^{*}
,

(\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\Omega_{\sigma}=(1+O(|z|))\Omega_{TY_{\sigma}}

(2.77)

as
|z|\to 0
, and the implied constant is independent of
\sigma
.

Proof.

There exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that for every fixed
\sigma\in\Delta^{*}
, the function
H_{\sigma}:=[(\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\Omega_{\sigma}]/\Omega_{TY_{\sigma}}
is holomorphic on
TY_{\sigma}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(0)
. Since all data depend holomorphically on
\sigma
, it follows that

H:B_{\varepsilon}(0)\setminus TY_{0}\to\mathbb{C},\;\,z\mapsto H_{\sigma}(z)\;\,\text{with}\;\,\sigma=z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}+z_{3}^{3}\neq 0,

(2.78)

is holomorphic.

Claim 2.20
.

H
extends holomorphically to
B_{\varepsilon}(0)
.

Proof of Claim
2.20
.

It is enough to show that
H
extends holomorphically to
B_{\varepsilon}(0)\setminus\{0\}
because then the claim follows from Hartogs’ theorem. Obviously the numerator,
(\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\Omega_{\sigma}
, is holomorphic even at the points of
TY_{0}\setminus\{0\}
. For the denominator,
\Omega_{TY_{\sigma}}
, cover
\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
by the open sets
\{z_{i}\neq 0\}

(i=1,2,3)
. By symmetry we only need to consider the case
i=1
. By adjunction, there exists
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
\Omega_{TY_{1}}
is the restriction to the tangent bundle of
TY_{1}
of the
2
-form
\gamma(dz_{2}\wedge dz_{3})/z_{1}^{2}
on
\{z_{1}\neq 0\}
. The latter is invariant under
m_{\sigma}
. Thus,
\Omega_{TY_{\sigma}}
is given by the same formula, so it extends as a holomorphic and nowhere vanishing section of the relative canonical bundle across
TY_{0}\setminus\{0\}
.
∎

It remains to show that
H(0)=1
. To see that this is true, we approach the origin along a sequence of points in
TY_{0}\setminus\{0\}
. Along this sequence,
[(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\Omega_{0}]/\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1
by the normalization chosen after the proof of Lemma
2.12
, so we need to show that
\Omega_{TY_{0}}/\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1
as well. In fact,
\Omega_{TY_{0}}=\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
on
TY_{0}\setminus\{0\}
. The reason is that these forms are scale-invariant on the cone
TY_{0}\setminus\{0\}
and have the same residue at the infinity divisor
E
; compare the general construction of
\Omega_{X}
before Theorem
2.16
.
∎

2.4.
A new neck region between a Tian-Yau end and a hyperbolic cusp

We now return to the radial Kähler-Einstein equation (
2.42
) on a general
n
-dimensional cusp singularity, which already gave us the asymptotic model
\psi_{cusp}
for the unique complete Kähler-Einstein potential on the regular part of our
2
-dimensional example
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
In general, (
2.42
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}(\psi^{\prime})^{n+1}=e^{\psi+a}+b

(2.79)

for some constant
b
.
Then we have, for any
t<t_{0},

\displaystyle\int_{\psi(t)}^{\psi(t_{0})}(e^{\xi+a}+b)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}d\xi=(n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}(t_{0}-t).

(2.80)

The case
b=0
yields the cusp solution
\psi_{cusp}
. The case
b>0
yields solutions that are still defined on an entire negative half-line but are not metrically complete as
t\to-\infty
. In
[
4
, Sect 2]
and
[
13
, Ex 2.7]
, it was shown that the completions of these metrics may be viewed as Kähler-Einstein edge metrics on the total space of
L
, with conical singularities along the zero section that get pushed off to infinity as
b\to 0
while the diameter of the zero section shrinks to zero. Since we are now considering smoothings rather than resolutions of singularities, it is natural to try to use the case
b<0
.

We showed in
[
13
]
that when
b<0,
there are solutions to (
2.80
) which correspond to incomplete Kähler-Einstein metrics on
\{\delta^{-}<h\leqslant\delta^{+}\}
, where
\delta^{-},\delta^{+}>0
are constants that depend on
a,b
. In fact, (
2.41
) and (
2.79
) imply that
e^{\psi+a}+b>0
. When
b<0,
the integral on the left-hand side of (
2.80
) is bounded and the bound is independent of
t,t_{0}
. The purpose of this section is to study these solutions in detail. They give rise to “horn metrics”
\omega_{T}
approximating
\omega_{cusp}
as in Figure
2
.

Convention 2.21
.

We will introduce geometric parameters
T<T+2T_{0}<2\tau<0
, and
b,T,T_{0},\tau
will eventually be made to depend on
\sigma
. The standing assumption is that as
\sigma\to 0
we have that

\displaystyle T\to-\infty,\;\,T_{0}\to+\infty,\;\,T_{0}/T\to 0,\;\,\tau\to-\infty,\;\,\tau/T\to 0,\;\,b\to 0,\;\,b|\tau|^{n+1}\to 0.

(2.81)

We will be proving many
O(\ldots)
type estimates for various different quantities, and the understanding will always be that the
O(\ldots)
holds as
\sigma\to 0
, so that (
2.81
) is in force.

We assume
\psi_{T}(t)
is the solution of (
2.42
) such that

\displaystyle e^{\psi_{T}(T)+a}+b=0,\quad\psi_{T}(\tau)=\psi_{cusp}(\tau).

(2.82)

Then

\displaystyle\psi_{T}(T)=\log|b|-a,\quad\psi_{T}(\tau)=-(n+1)\log(-\tau)+n\log(n+1)-a.

(2.83)

We note here that with
\omega_{T}:=i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{T}
it holds that

e^{-\psi_{T}}\omega_{T}^{n}=i^{n^{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}.

(2.84)

Indeed, radial pluriharmonic functions are constant (cf. (
2.48
)), so there is a constant
C_{T}
such that

e^{-\psi_{T}+C_{T}}\omega_{T}^{n}=i^{n^{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}.

(2.85)

Now by taking
t=\tau
and noting that
\psi_{T}(\tau)=\psi_{cusp}(\tau)
(cf. (
2.82
)), one has

i^{n^{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}=e^{-\psi_{T}(\tau)+C_{T}}\omega_{T}^{n}=e^{-\psi_{cusp}(\tau)+C_{T}}\omega_{T}^{n}=e^{C_{T}}i^{n^{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}

(2.86)

as before. So indeed
C_{T}=0
.

Figure 2.
Cusp metric
\omega_{cusp}
, horn metric
\omega_{T}
, gluing regions.

Remark 2.22
.

We show the relationship between
\omega_{T}
and
\omega_{cusp}
in Figure
2
. The metric
\omega_{T}
has a horn singularity at
t=T
, which evolves into the cusp singularity as
T\to-\infty
. Later we will see that for a fixed
T
, the horn of
\omega_{T}
is asymptotic to a scaled copy of the Calabi model space, which allows us to carry out a gluing construction in the region
t\in[T+T_{0},T+2T_{0}]
that we shaded orange in the figure (
T_{0}\to+\infty
but
T_{0}/T\to 0
). Similarly, in the green region,
t\in[2\tau,\tau]
(
\tau\to-\infty
but
\tau/T\to 0
), we can glue
\omega_{T}
with
\omega_{cusp}
. We often refer to the region
T+2T_{0}<t<2\tau
as the “new” or “middle” neck.

By (
2.80
),

\displaystyle\int_{\psi_{T}(T)}^{\psi_{T}(\tau)}(e^{\xi+a}+b)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}d\xi=(n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}(\tau-T).

(2.87)

(
2.80
) and (
2.83
) show that, by setting
\xi=s+\log|b|-a
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{-(n+1)\log|\tau|-\log|b|+n\log(n+1)}(e^{s}-1)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds=(n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}(\tau-T).

(2.88)

Hence we obtain the crucial relation between
T
and
b
:

\displaystyle T=-(n+1)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}c(n)|b|^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}+O(\tau),

(2.89)

where

\displaystyle c(n):=\int_{0}^{\infty}(e^{s}-1)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds.

(2.90)

We conclude that

\displaystyle\psi_{cusp}(T)=\log|b|-(n+1)\log c(n)+O(|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\tau).

(2.91)

Hence,

\displaystyle\psi_{cusp}(T)-\psi_{T}(T)=-(n+1)\log c(n)+a+O(|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\tau),

(2.92)

which tends to a constant as
\sigma\to 0
by (
2.81
).

2.4.1.
Estimates asymptotically close to the cusp (green region)

Similarly to (
2.80
), one has

\displaystyle\int_{\psi_{T}(t)}^{\psi_{T}(\tau)}(e^{\xi+a}+b)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}d\xi=(n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}(\tau-t).

(2.93)

Substitute
\xi=s+\psi_{T}(\tau)
.
By (
2.83
),

\displaystyle\int_{\psi_{T}(t)-\psi_{T}(\tau)}^{0}(e^{s}+(n+1)^{-n}b|\tau|^{n+1})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds=-(n+1)(1-\tau^{-1}t).

(2.94)

Let
c_{1}>0
be the unique constant such that

\displaystyle\int_{-c_{1}}^{0}(e^{s})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds=n+1.

(2.95)

When
t=2\tau
, the right-hand side of (
2.94
) equals
n+1
. As
b<0
, we have

\displaystyle(e^{s}+e^{-a}b|\tau|^{n+1})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}>(e^{s})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}>0.

(2.96)

By comparing (
2.94
) and (
2.95
),
we derive that
\psi_{T}(2\tau)-\psi_{T}(\tau)>-c_{1}
. By taking
b\rightarrow 0^{-}
in (
2.94
), we see that
\psi_{T}(t)-\psi_{T}(\tau)
must decrease to a
constant. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\int_{\psi_{cusp}(2\tau)-\psi_{cusp}(\tau)}^{0}(e^{s})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds=n+1.

(2.97)

By (
2.81
) and all the discussions above,

\psi_{T}(2\tau)-\psi_{T}(\tau)
converges to

\psi_{cusp}(2\tau)-\psi_{cusp}(\tau).

By (
2.82
),
\psi_{T}(2\tau)\rightarrow\psi_{cusp}(2\tau)
.
Replacing
2\tau
by any constant in
(2\tau,\tau)
, the same argument implies that,

\displaystyle\psi_{T}(t)-\psi_{cusp}(t)=o(1)

(2.98)

as
b\rightarrow 0^{-}
if we fix
\frac{t}{\tau}
as a constant in
[1,2]
.

When
t=c\tau
for some constant
c\in[1,2]
,

s\geqslant\psi_{T}(t)-\psi_{T}(\tau)\geqslant-c_{1}
in (
2.94
). Using that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\psi_{T}(t)-\psi_{T}(\tau)}^{0}(e^{s}+(n+1)^{-n}b|\tau|^{n+1})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds&=(n+1)(c-1)\\
&=\int_{\psi_{cusp}(t)-\psi_{cusp}(\tau)}^{0}(e^{s})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds,\end{split}

(2.99)

we derive

\displaystyle\int_{\psi_{T}(t)-\psi_{T}(\tau)}^{0}\left((e^{s}+(n+1)^{-n}b|\tau|^{n+1})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}-(e^{s})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}\right)ds=\int_{\psi_{cusp}(t)-\psi_{T}(t)}^{0}(e^{s})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds,

(2.100)

which shows that, for
t\in[2\tau,\tau]
,

\displaystyle\psi_{T}(t)-\psi_{cusp}(t)=O(|b||\tau|^{n+1}).

(2.101)

Let
\rho:=\sqrt{(n+1)/2}\log(-t)
, which is a Busemann function of
\omega_{cusp}
.
For
t\in[2\tau,\tau]
,
\rho
varies by an additive constant independent of
b
. Set the normalized Busemann function

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}(t):=\rho(t)-\rho(\tau)=\sqrt{\frac{n+1}{2}}\log\left(\frac{t}{\tau}\right),

(2.102)

whose range is

I=[0,\sqrt{(n+1)/2}\log 2].
Regard
t\psi_{T}^{\prime},t^{2}\psi_{T}^{\prime\prime}
as functions of
\tilde{\rho}
.
As
b\rightarrow 0^{-},

\displaystyle\begin{split}t\psi_{T}^{\prime}&=t(n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}(e^{\psi_{T}+a}+b)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\\
&=-e^{\sqrt{\frac{2}{n+1}}\tilde{\rho}}(n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}(e^{\psi_{T}-\psi_{T}(\tau)+a}+b|\tau|^{n+1})^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\\
&=-(n+1)+O(|b||\tau|^{n+1}),\end{split}

(2.103)

uniformly for
\tilde{\rho}\in I
,
where we have applied the fact that

\displaystyle\psi_{T}-\psi_{T}(\tau)=\psi_{cusp}-\psi_{cusp}(\tau)+O(|b||\tau|^{n+1})=-(n+1)\log|t/\tau|+O(|b||\tau|^{n+1}).

(2.104)

A similar computation shows that

\displaystyle t^{2}\psi_{T}^{\prime\prime}=t^{2}e^{\psi_{T}+a}(\psi_{T}^{\prime})^{1-n}=e^{\psi_{T}-\psi_{cusp}+n\log(n+1)}(-t\psi_{T}^{\prime})^{1-n}=n+1+O(|b||\tau|^{n+1})

(2.105)

as
b\rightarrow 0^{-}
. By differentiating

\displaystyle t^{2}\psi_{T}^{\prime\prime}=(n+1)^{n}e^{\psi_{T}-\psi_{cusp}}(-t\psi_{T}^{\prime})^{1-n}

(2.106)

multiple times
and multiplying by
t
each time, we derive the following lemma.

Lemma 2.23
.

For
k\geqslant 1,

\displaystyle t^{k}\psi_{T}^{(k)}=(-1)^{k}(n+1)(k-1)!+O_{k}(|b||\tau|^{n+1})

(2.107)

on
\{\tilde{\rho}\in I\}
as
b\rightarrow 0^{-}
. Here
(-1)^{k}(n+1)(k-1)!=t^{k}\psi_{cusp}^{(k)}
.

Introduce a local bundle chart
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
on the total space of
L
such that
z_{n}=0
cuts out the zero section. Write the Hermitian metric as
h=e^{-\varphi}|z_{n}|^{2}
, where
\varphi
depends only on
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n-1}
. Then for any radial potential
\psi=\psi(t)
,
t=\log h
, the associated
(1,1)
-form can be written as

\displaystyle\omega=i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi=-\psi^{\prime}i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi+\psi^{\prime\prime}i(\partial\varphi-\partial\log z_{n})\wedge(\overline{\partial}\varphi-\overline{\partial}\log\overline{z}_{n}).

(2.108)

We now assume that the Calabi-Yau
(E,\omega_{E})
,
\omega_{E}=-i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi
, is flat and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n-1}
are the standard linear coordinates on the universal cover
\mathbb{C}^{n-1}\to E
. We also write
z_{\alpha}=x_{\alpha}+iy_{\alpha}
for
1\leqslant\alpha\leqslant n-1
,
\theta={\rm arg}\,z_{n}
and
x=-1/t
. Then, on the universal cover of
\{2\tau\leqslant t\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\tau\}
, we define a new chart via

\displaystyle(\check{x}_{\alpha},\check{y}_{\alpha},\check{x},\check{\theta}):=(|\tau|^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{\alpha},|\tau|^{-\frac{1}{2}}y_{\alpha},|\tau|x,|\tau|^{-1}\theta)

(2.109)

which takes the value
p=(0,\ldots,0,1,0)
at a point
q
such that
t(q)=\tau
. Under this chart, the metric
\omega_{cusp}
is equivalent to
\delta_{ij}
and in addition, for all
k,\alpha
,

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}_{cusp}i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi,\quad\psi^{\prime\prime}_{cusp}i(\partial\varphi-\partial\log z_{n})\wedge(\overline{\partial}\varphi-\overline{\partial}\log\overline{z}_{n})

(2.110)

have bounded
C^{k}
norms on a fixed size ball centered at
p
, with bounds that are independent of
\tau
. Also,
\psi_{T},\psi_{cusp}
are independent of
x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha},\theta.

Applying this chart, Lemma
2.23
implies that, for

t\in[2\tau,\tau],

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{k}(\psi_{T}-\psi_{cusp})|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O_{k}(|b||\tau|^{n+1}).

(2.111)

Meanwhile, to prove higher order derivative estimates of
\omega_{T}
under the coordinates (
2.109
), we only have to check that

\displaystyle\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{T}}{\psi^{\prime}_{cusp}},\;\,\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}_{T}}{\psi^{\prime\prime}_{cusp}}

(2.112)

have uniformly bounded
C^{k}
norm with respect to (
2.109
). For example,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\check{x}}\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}_{T}}{\psi^{\prime\prime}_{cusp}}=-|\tau|^{-1}\frac{\partial t}{\partial x}\cdot\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{T}\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime}-\psi^{\prime\prime}_{T}\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime\prime}}{(\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime})^{2}},

(2.113)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}t^{5}(\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{T}\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime}-\psi^{\prime\prime}_{T}\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime\prime})=(t^{3}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{T}-t^{3}\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime\prime})t^{2}\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime}-(t^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}_{T}-t^{2}\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime})t^{3}\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime\prime},\end{split}

(2.114)

which is
O(|b||\tau|^{n+1})
by Lemma
2.23
. This shows that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\check{x}}\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}_{T}}{\psi^{\prime\prime}_{cusp}}=O(|b||\tau|^{n+1}).

(2.115)

We can estimate the
C^{k}
norm of (
2.112
) with
k\geqslant 1
by induction, using the formula

\displaystyle\left(\frac{f}{g}\right)^{(k)}=\frac{1}{g}\left(f^{(k)}-k!\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{g^{(k+1-j)}}{(k+1-j)!}\frac{\left(\frac{f}{g}\right)^{(j-1)}}{(j-1)!}\right)

(2.116)

with

\displaystyle f=\psi^{\prime}_{T}-\psi^{\prime}_{cusp},\;\,g=\psi^{\prime}_{cusp}

(2.117)

or with

\displaystyle f=\psi^{\prime\prime}_{T}-\psi^{\prime\prime}_{cusp},\;\,g=\psi^{\prime\prime}_{cusp}.

(2.118)

By Lemma
2.23
and (
2.44
), we have for any
k\geqslant 1
that
f^{(k)}=O(|b||\tau|^{n+1})
.

Thus, in conclusion, we have proved the following lemma.

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
\psi_{T}
be the solution of (
2.42
) satisfying (
2.88
) and assume that (
2.81
) is in force.
Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and for all
t\in[2\tau,\tau]
,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{k}(\omega_{T}-\omega_{cusp})|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O(|b||\tau|^{n+1}).

(2.119)

2.4.2.
Estimates on the middle neck

Proposition 2.25
.

Assume that
b\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)
. Let
\psi_{T}
be the solution of (
2.42
) satisfying (
2.88
).
Then:

(1)

For
t\in[T,\tau],

\displaystyle 0\leqslant\psi_{T}(t)-\psi_{cusp}(t)<C,

(2.120)

where
C
does not depend on
b
.

(2)

\psi_{T}(t)-\psi_{cusp}(t)
is a decreasing function on
[T,\tau]
.

(3)

\psi_{T}^{\prime}(t)<\psi_{cusp}^{\prime}(t)
for
t\in[T,\tau]
.

Proof.

By (
2.82
), (
2.92
),
when
t=\tau
or
T
,
0\leqslant\psi_{T}-\psi_{cusp}<C
, where
C
is independent of
b
. Now assume that
\psi_{T}-\psi_{cusp}
has a positive local maximum at
p\in(T,\tau)
. Then

\displaystyle\psi_{T}(p)>\psi_{cusp}(p),

(2.121)

\displaystyle 0<\psi_{T}^{\prime}(p)=\psi_{cusp}^{\prime}(p),

(2.122)

\displaystyle 0<\psi_{T}^{\prime\prime}(p)\leqslant\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime}(p),

(2.123)

which contradicts the fact that
\psi_{T}
and
\psi_{cusp}
both satisfy equation (
2.42
).
Similarly,
\psi_{T}-\psi_{cusp}
does not have a negative local minimum in
(T,\log|b|)
. Part (1) of the lemma is proved.

Notice that

\psi_{T}-\psi_{cusp}=0
at
\tau
. If
\psi_{T}-\psi_{cusp}
is not monotone, then it has either a positive local maximum or a negative local minimum in
(T,\tau)
, which contradicts the above discussion. At
\tau
,
\psi_{T}^{\prime}<\psi_{cusp}^{\prime}
by (
2.79
). So
\psi_{T}-\psi_{cusp}
is a decreasing function.

For part (3), we assume that it is not true and set

\displaystyle t_{0}:=\sup\{t\in[T,\tau]:\psi_{T}^{\prime}(t)\geqslant\psi^{\prime}_{cusp}(t)\}.

(2.124)

Then
t_{0}<\tau
. At
t_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle 0<\psi_{T}^{\prime}(t_{0})=\psi_{cusp}^{\prime}(t_{0}),

(2.125)

\displaystyle 0<\psi_{T}^{\prime\prime}(t_{0})\leqslant\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime}(t_{0}),

(2.126)

By part (2),
\psi_{T}(t_{0})>\psi_{cusp}(t_{0})
. This contradicts equation (
2.42
).
∎

Proposition 2.26
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
. Then for all
\eta\in(0,\delta]
we have that

\displaystyle\psi_{T}(\eta T)=\psi_{cusp}(\eta T)+O(|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\tau)+O_{\delta}(\eta^{n+1}).

(2.127)

In addition,

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}_{T}(\eta T)

\displaystyle=\psi_{cusp}^{\prime}(\eta T)e^{O_{\delta}(\eta^{n+1})}(1+O(|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\tau)),

(2.128)

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime\prime}_{T}(\eta T)

\displaystyle=\psi_{cusp}^{\prime\prime}(\eta T)e^{O_{\delta}(\eta^{n+1})}(1+O(|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\tau)).

(2.129)

Proof.

Taking
t_{0}=\eta T
and
t=T
and substituting
\xi=s+\psi_{T}(T)
in (
2.80
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\psi_{T}(\eta T)-\psi_{T}(T)}(e^{s}-1)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds=(n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}(\eta T-T).

(2.130)

By part (2) of Proposition
2.25
, as
\eta\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle\psi_{T}(\eta T)-\psi_{T}(T)>\psi_{cusp}(\eta T)-\psi_{cusp}(T)-C\rightarrow\infty.

(2.131)

Taking
\eta\rightarrow 0^{+}
in (
2.130
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(e^{s}-1)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds=(n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}|T|.

(2.132)

Subtracting it from (
2.130
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\psi_{T}(\eta T)-\psi_{T}(T)}^{\infty}(e^{s}-1)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds&=(n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\eta|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}|T|\\
&=\eta\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}(e^{s}-1)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds+O(|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\tau)\right),\end{split}

(2.133)

where we applied (
2.89
) for the last step.
When
s>0
is bounded away from 0,

\displaystyle(e^{s}-1)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}=e^{-\frac{s}{n+1}}(1-e^{-s})^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}=e^{-\frac{s}{n+1}}+O(e^{-\frac{(n+2)s}{n+1}}).

(2.134)

Thus,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{T}(\eta T)-\psi_{T}(T)&=(n+1)\log(n+1)-(n+1)\log\eta\\
&-(n+1)\log\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}(e^{s}-1)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds\right)+O(|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\tau)+O(\eta^{n+1}).\end{split}

(2.135)

Comparing this to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{cusp}(\eta T)-\psi_{T}(T)&=-(n+1)\log(-\eta T)-\log|b|+n\log(n+1)\\
&=-(n+1)\log\eta+(n+1)\log(n+1)\\
&-(n+1)\log\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}(e^{s}-1)^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}ds\right)+O(|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\tau),\end{split}

(2.136)

we obtain that

\displaystyle\psi_{T}(\eta T)-\psi_{cusp}(\eta T)=O(|b|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\tau)+O(\eta^{n+1}).

(2.137)

The rest of the proposition follows by analyzing (
2.79
) and (
2.42
).
∎

Definition 2.27

(Perturbation of differential operators)
.

Given a function
\varepsilon(\mathbf{x})>0
on a local chart
\mathbf{x}
, we say that
f(\mathbf{x})=O(\varepsilon(\mathbf{x}))
if
|f(\mathbf{x})|\leqslant C\varepsilon(\mathbf{x})
for some
C>0
independent of
\mathbf{x}
and of
b,T
.
Let
