|b|^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t-T)^{-\frac{3}{2}}

|b|^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t-T)^{-\frac{3}{2}}

|b|^{-\frac{1}{2}}

Figure 3.
The seven regions of
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
. For the middle neck
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
see also Figure
2
.

Definition 3.1
.

We now define our glued approximate Kähler-Einstein metric
\omega_{glue,\sigma}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
. On the affine surface
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}=\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
we set
\omega_{glue,\sigma}:=i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{glue,\sigma}
, where
\psi_{glue,\sigma}
is defined as follows. We need to distinguish
7
different regions
\mathfrak{R}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{R}_{7}
; see Table
1
and Figure
3
. We begin by setting

\psi_{glue,\sigma}:=\psi_{FS,\sigma}+(G_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{0}\;\,\text{on}\;\,\mathfrak{R}_{1}:=\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}(\Phi_{\sigma}(\{t>-N\}))\cup(\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}\setminus{\rm dom}\,\Psi_{\sigma}).

(3.4)

Here
\psi_{FS,\sigma}
was defined in (
2.31
),
\psi_{0}=\psi_{KE,0}-\psi_{FS,0}
(Lemma
2.9
), and
G_{\sigma}
is the
C^{5}
diffeomorphism from Lemma
2.7
, while
N
is an arbitrary but fixed large positive constant such that
\Phi_{\sigma}(\{t<-N/2\})
is contained in
{\rm dom}\,\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}
. Clearly
\omega_{glue,\sigma}
is then at least
C^{3}
at the divisor
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
.

For the remaining
6
regions we prefer to write down formulas for
(\Psi^{-1}_{\sigma})^{*}\psi_{glue,\sigma}
on
TY_{\sigma}
rather than for
\psi_{glue,\sigma}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
. To this end, let
\chi_{1}(t)
be smooth and increasing with
\chi_{1}(t)=0
for
t\leqslant T+T_{0}
and
\chi_{1}(t)=1
for
t\geqslant T+2T_{0}
, and with
|\partial_{t}^{j}\chi_{1}|=O_{j}(T_{0}^{-j})
for all
j\geqslant 0
. Similarly,
let
\chi_{2}(t)
be smooth and increasing with
\chi_{2}(t)=0
for
t\leqslant 2\tau
and
\chi_{2}(t)=1
for
t\geqslant\tau
, and with
|\partial_{t}^{j}\chi_{2}|=O_{j}(|\tau|^{-j})
for all
j\geqslant 0
. Then the desired formulas for
(\Psi^{-1}_{\sigma})^{*}\psi_{glue,\sigma}
are as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\Psi^{-1}_{\sigma})^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}+((\Psi_{\sigma}\circ G_{\sigma})^{-1})^{*}\psi_{0}&\;\,\text{on}\;\,\Psi_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{R}_{2}):=\Phi_{\sigma}(\{\tau<t<-N\}),\\
(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\Big(\chi_{2}(\Psi^{-1}_{0})^{*}(G_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}+\psi_{0})+(1-\chi_{2})\psi_{T}\Big)&\;\,\text{on}\;\,\Psi_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{R}_{3}):=\Phi_{\sigma}(\{2\tau<t<\tau\}),\\
(\Phi^{-1}_{\sigma})^{*}\psi_{T}&\;\,\text{on}\;\,\Psi_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{R}_{4}):=\Phi_{\sigma}(\{T+2T_{0}<t<2\tau\}),\\
(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\Big(\chi_{1}\psi_{T}+(1-\chi_{1})\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi_{TY_{\sigma}}+\psi_{T}(T))\Big)&\;\,\text{on}\;\,\Psi_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{R}_{5}):=\Phi_{\sigma}(\{T_{0}<t-T<2T_{0}\}),\\
\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi_{TY_{\sigma}}+\psi_{T}(T)&\;\,\text{on}\;\,\Psi_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{R}_{6}):=\Phi_{\sigma}(\{t<T+T_{0}\}),\\
\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi_{TY_{\sigma}}+\psi_{T}(T)&\;\,\text{on}\;\,\Psi_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{R}_{7}):=({\rm dom}\,\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1})\setminus({\rm im}\,\Phi_{\sigma}).\end{split}

(3.5)

This concludes our definition of the glued approximate Kähler-Einstein manifold
(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma},\omega_{glue,\sigma})
.

The Monge-Ampère equation that we want to solve is

(\omega_{glue,\sigma}+i\partial\overline{\partial}u_{\sigma})^{2}=e^{u_{\sigma}-f_{\sigma}}\omega_{glue,\sigma}^{2},

(3.6)

where
f_{\sigma}
is the Ricci potential, defined up to a constant by the condition that

\displaystyle{\rm Ric}(\omega_{glue,\sigma})+\omega_{glue,\sigma}+i\partial\overline{\partial}f_{\sigma}=0.

(3.7)

Lemma 3.2
.

Up to an arbitrary constant,

f_{\sigma}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}}=\log\left(\frac{\omega_{glue,\sigma}^{2}}{\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}\right)-\psi_{glue,\sigma}.

(3.8)

Proof.

Denote the right-hand side of (
3.8
) by
g_{\sigma}
.

Claim 3.3
.

g_{\sigma}
extends at least
C^{3}
to
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
.

Proof of Claim
3.3
.

We know that
\omega_{glue,\sigma}^{2}
is at least
C^{3}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
and
\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}=|H_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}}|^{4}\cdot(\textit{smooth on $\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}$})
, where
H_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}}
is a defining section of the line bundle
[\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}]
and
|\cdot|
is any smooth Hermitian metric on this line bundle. Thus, the log volume ratio in (
3.8
) is of the form
\log|H_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}}|^{-4}+(\textit{at least $C^{3}$ on $\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}$})
. Finally, since the reference Kähler form
\omega_{FS,\sigma}
represents the Poincaré dual of the divisor class
[2\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}]
, we have that
\psi_{glue,\sigma}=\psi_{FS,\sigma}+(\textit{at least $C^{5}$ on $\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}$})=\log|H_{\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}}|^{-4}+(\textit{at least $C^{5}$ on $\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}$})
. Thus, the two log terms in the definition of
g_{\sigma}
cancel each other out and the remainder is at least
C^{3}
.
∎

From its definition, (
3.7
), and by elliptic regularity,
f_{\sigma}
is at least
C^{2,\alpha}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
. Hence, by Claim
3.3
, so is
f_{\sigma}-g_{\sigma}
. From the definition of
g_{\sigma}
and from the standard formula for the Ricci curvature of a Kähler metric,
g_{\sigma}
satisfies
{\rm Ric}(\omega_{glue,\sigma})+\omega_{glue,\sigma}+i\partial\overline{\partial}g_{\sigma}=0
(on
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}
and thus, by Claim
3.3
, on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
). Thus,
f_{\sigma}-g_{\sigma}
is pluriharmonic on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
and hence constant.
∎

Of course, we already know that, given
f_{\sigma}
, the Monge-Ampère equation (
3.6
) has a unique solution
u_{\sigma}
by the Aubin-Yau theorem
[
2
,
40
]
, and if we change
f_{\sigma}
by a constant then
u_{\sigma}
changes by the same constant. Our main goal in this paper is to prove that
u_{\sigma}
is actually small modulo constants, meaning at the very least that
\sup_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}|i\partial\overline{\partial}u_{\sigma}|_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}\to 0
as
\sigma\to 0
. Clearly, the first step here is to prove that
f_{\sigma}
is sufficiently small modulo constants, using the expression in (
3.8
). This estimate is the main result of Section
3
and we record it in Theorem
3.5
. To state the theorem, we need one other definition.

Definition 3.4
.

The regularity scale function
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}:\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
of
\omega_{glue,\sigma}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}:=\begin{cases}1&\text{on}\;\,\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}(\Phi_{\sigma}(\{t>T/2\}))\cup(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\setminus{\rm dom}\,\Psi_{\sigma}),\\
\Psi_{\sigma}^{*}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\left(|b|^{\frac{1}{4}}(t-T)^{\frac{3}{4}}\right)&\text{on}\;\,\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}(\Phi_{\sigma}(\{t<T/2\})),\\
|b|^{\frac{1}{4}}&\text{on}\;\,\mathfrak{R}_{7}.\end{cases}

(3.9)

Up to bounded factors that we suppress,
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}(p)
is the maximal radius of an
\omega_{glue,\sigma}
-ball
B_{r}(p)
such that
r^{-2}\omega_{glue,\sigma}
is
C^{\infty}
bounded in coordinates on the universal cover of
B_{r}(p)
. In particular, the curvature of
\omega_{glue,\sigma}
is uniformly
O(\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}^{-2})
. This is the curvature estimate recorded in Table
1
and it is sharp.

We are now able to state our main result in this section. The proof is deferred to Section
3.2
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
f_{\sigma}
be the Ricci potential defined in (
3.8
). Then for all
\varepsilon>0
the function

|f_{\sigma}|+\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}|\nabla_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}f_{\sigma}|_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}

(3.10)

satisfies the following pointwise estimates as
\sigma\to 0
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}O(|\sigma|)&\textnormal{on $\mathfrak{R}_{1}$},\\
O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})&\textnormal{on $\mathfrak{R}_{2}$},\\
O(|b||\tau|^{3})+O(e^{-\delta_{0}\sqrt{-t}})&\textnormal{on $\mathfrak{R}_{3}$},\\
O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})+O(e^{(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)t})&\textnormal{on $\mathfrak{R}_{4}$},\\
O((T_{0}/|T|)^{\frac{3}{2}})&\textnormal{on $\mathfrak{R}_{5}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{6}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{7}$}.\end{cases}

(3.11)

Here we abuse notation by writing
t
instead of the correct
\Psi_{\sigma}^{*}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}t
.

Remark 3.6
.

The shape of (
3.11
) makes sense intuitively:
f_{\sigma}
decays faster than any polynomial in
|T|
on
\mathfrak{R}_{1}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{2}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{4}
because
\omega_{glue,\sigma}
is almost an exact solution of the negative Kähler-Einstein equation in these regions. On the other hand, on
\mathfrak{R}_{5}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{6}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{7}
we are gluing with the scaled Tian-Yau metric, which is Ricci-flat, so up to some additive constant
f_{\sigma}
then equals minus the Kähler potential of this metric, whose oscillation is
\sim

(T_{0}/|T|)^{3/2}
. The error in the gluing region
\mathfrak{R}_{3}
is also polynomial in
|T|
but it is
O(|T|^{-3}|{\log|T|}|^{6})
, i.e., almost quadratic compared to the error in the Tian-Yau region.

Remark 3.7
.

Using only (
3.11
) and standard facts from the theory of the complex Monge-Ampère equation, one can already deduce quite a bit of information about the solution
u_{\sigma}
. For example, the maximum principle applied to (
3.6
) immediately yields that

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}|u_{\sigma}|\leqslant\sup\nolimits_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}|f_{\sigma}|=O(|b|^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}).

(3.12)

On
\mathfrak{R}_{1}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{2}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{3}
and on a large portion of the middle neck
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
, the regularity scale
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}
is uniformly bounded below, i.e., we have uniform
C^{\infty}
bounds for
\omega_{glue,\sigma}
on the universal cover of a geodesic ball of definite size centered at any point. Thus, on all of these regions,
|i\partial\overline{\partial}u_{\sigma}|_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}=O(|b|^{(1-\alpha)/2})
by combining (
3.12
) and Savin’s small perturbation theorem
[
32
, Thm 1.3]
. This estimate already implies
C^{\infty}_{loc}
convergence of
\omega_{KE,\sigma}
to
\omega_{KE,0}
away from any fixed neighborhood of the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

On the other hand, moving towards the left boundary of the middle neck
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
and into
\mathfrak{R}_{5}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{6}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{7}
, the regularity scale
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}
decays until it reaches its minimum of
|b|^{1/4}
on the Tian-Yau cap
\mathfrak{R}_{7}
. To be able to apply Savin’s theorem in this situation we would need that
|u_{\sigma}|\ll\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}^{2}
, i.e.,
|u_{\sigma}|\ll|b|^{1/2}
on
\mathfrak{R}_{7}
. This is obviously out of reach of (
3.12
) no matter how small we make
\alpha
.

The point of the weighted Hölder space theory developed in the rest of the paper (after the proof of Theorem
3.5
) is precisely to improve the naive
C^{0}
estimate (
3.12
) in the Tian-Yau region. We will for instance be able to prove that
\sup_{\mathfrak{R}_{7}}|u_{\sigma}|=O_{\varepsilon}(|b|^{(5/6)-\varepsilon})
for all
\varepsilon>0
. This is enough to obtain
C^{1,\beta}
closeness of
\omega_{KE,\sigma}
to the scaled Tian-Yau metric on
\mathfrak{R}_{7}
for all
\beta<\frac{1}{3}
, which in particular proves the Main Theorem. In fact, we conjecture that a more systematic approach to the obstruction theory in Section
5.2
would even yield
\sup_{\mathfrak{R}_{7}}|u_{\sigma}|=O_{\varepsilon}(|b|^{1-\varepsilon})
and thus
C^{1,\beta}
closeness for all
\beta<1
. This would then be optimal because a Ricci-flat metric cannot be
C^{1,1}
close to a metric with Ricci
=

-1
.

3.2.
Proof of the Ricci potential estimate

This section is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
3.5
. This will be done at the end of this section, as a consequence of a long sequence of lemmas.

We shall pull back everything back to
TY_{0}
and then estimate.

Lemma 3.8
.

On
TY_{0}
, the following hold.

(1)

If
t\in[T+T_{0},\tau]
, one has

|\nabla_{\omega_{T}}^{k}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}J_{TY_{\sigma}}-J_{\mathcal{C}})|_{\omega_{T}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})\;\,\text{for all}\;\,k\geqslant 0,\varepsilon>0.

(3.13)

(2)

If
t\in[2\tau,-N]
, one has

|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{k}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}J_{TY_{\sigma}}-J_{\mathcal{C}})|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})\;\,\text{for all}\;\,k\geqslant 0,\varepsilon>0.

(3.14)

Proof.

In Lemma
2.18
we have proved that this holds for the reference metric
|b|^{1/2}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}
instead of
\omega_{T}
or
\omega_{cusp}
, assuming only that
t-T\to\infty
. To compare these reference metrics, we now estimate

\left|\nabla^{k}_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}\omega_{T}\right|_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}\,\,\,\textnormal{and}\,\,\,\left|\nabla^{k}_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}\omega_{cusp}\right|_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}\,\,\,\textnormal{for all}\,\,\,k\geqslant 0.

(3.15)

For
\delta>0
fixed and
t/T\geqslant 1-\delta
, (
2.164
)–(
2.165
) say that
\omega_{T}
is uniformly comparable to
|b|^{1/2}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}
. For
k>0
, first notice that by (
2.172
), the
t
-derivatives of
\psi_{T}
blow up at worst polynomially in
|T|
. Using the quasi-coordinates (
2.190
) for
\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}
and
|b|\sim|T|^{-3}
, we deduce that the
k
-th derivative of
\omega_{T}
with respect to
|b|^{1/2}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}
is bounded by
|T|^{N_{k}}
for some
N_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
. The exponential term
e^{-(1/2-\varepsilon)(t-T)}
absorbs these polynomial factors because
t-T\geqslant T_{0}=|T|^{\alpha}
. This proves (1) for
t/T\geqslant 1-\delta
.

Now assume
t/T\leqslant 1-\delta
and
t\leqslant-N
. We first compare
\omega_{T}
to
\omega_{cusp}
in a similar fashion, using Proposition
2.26
. Then we compare
\omega_{cusp}
to
|b|^{1/2}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}
using the fact that these metrics are explicit. This yields the remaining case of (1) (
t/T\leqslant 1-\delta
,
t\leqslant\tau
) and all cases of (2) (
2\tau\leqslant t\leqslant-N
).
∎

Similar arguments yield:

Lemma 3.9
.

On
TY_{0}
, the following hold.

(1)

If
t\in[T+T_{0},\tau]
, then for all
k\geqslant 0
there exists a positive integer
N_{k}
such that

|\nabla_{\omega_{T}}^{k}\psi_{T}|_{\omega_{T}}=O(|T|^{N_{k}}).

(3.16)

(2)

If
t\in[2\tau,-N]
, then for all
k\geqslant 0
there exists a positive integer
N_{k}
such that

|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{k}\psi_{cusp}|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O(|T|^{N_{k}}).

(3.17)

Now we combine Lemma
3.9
and Lemma
3.8
to get the following lemma, which essentially measures the non-holomorphicity of
\Phi_{\sigma}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

On
TY_{0}
, the following hold for all
k\geqslant 0
,
\varepsilon>0
.

(1)

If
t\in[T+T_{0},\tau]
, we have that

|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{T}}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{T}-\omega_{T})|_{\omega_{T}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.18)

(2)

If
t\in[2\tau,-N]
, we have that

|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{cusp}}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{cusp}-\omega_{cusp})|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.19)

Proof.

Let
A_{\sigma}:=\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}J_{\sigma}-J_{0}
. Then for all functions
f
on
TY_{\sigma}
and Kähler metrics
\omega
on
TY_{0}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}f-i\partial_{0}\overline{\partial}_{0}\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f&=\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}dd^{c}_{\sigma}f-dd^{c}_{0}\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f\\
&=d\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}J_{\sigma}df-dJ_{0}d\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f\\
&=d\Big((A_{\sigma}+J_{0})\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(df)\Big)-dJ_{0}d\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f\\
&=(\nabla_{\omega}A_{\sigma})\circledast(\nabla_{\omega}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f))+A_{\sigma}\circledast(\nabla^{2}_{\omega}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f)).\end{split}

(3.20)

Here
\circledast
denotes a tensorial contraction involving also the reference metric
\omega
. Now we apply this with
\omega=\omega_{T},\omega_{cusp}
and with
f=(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{T},(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{cusp}
, respectively. By Lemma
3.8
,
A_{\sigma}
and its covariant derivatives are bounded by
e^{-(1/2-\varepsilon)(t-T)}
. On the other hand,
\psi_{cusp},\psi_{T}
and their covariant derivatives blow up at worst polynomially in
|T|
by Lemma
3.9
. Since
t-T\geqslant T_{0}=|T|^{\alpha}
, these polynomial factors are again absorbed by the exponential decay as in the proof of Lemma
3.8
.
∎

The following auxiliary lemma will be used in Lemma
3.13
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Abuse notation by denoting
(\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}
by
\psi_{FS,\sigma}
for all sufficiently small values of
\sigma
, including for
\sigma=0
. This is a Kähler potential on the intersection of
TY_{\sigma}
with some fixed neighborhood of the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Let
z\in TY_{0}
with

\displaystyle|\sigma|^{\frac{1}{3}}R\leqslant|z|\leqslant\varepsilon,

(3.21)

where
\varepsilon
is a sufficiently small constant independent of
\sigma
and
R
is a large constant as in Lemma
2.5
. Let
\omega_{flat}:=i\partial\overline{\partial}|z|^{2}
be the flat metric on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Then for
k=0,1
and for
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\left|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}\left(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{FS,0}\right)\right|_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}=O(|\sigma||z|^{-3-k}),

(3.22)

\displaystyle\left|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}i\partial\overline{\partial}\left(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0}\right)\right|_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}=O(|\sigma||z|^{-3-k}).

(3.23)

Proof.

We only prove (
3.22
) as the proof of (
3.23
) is similar. To prove (
3.22
), we start with

Claim 3.12
.

One has

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{FS,0}=\biggl(\sum\nolimits_{|I|+|J|=2}\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-3})dz_{I}\wedge d\overline{z}_{J}\biggr)\biggr|_{TY_{0}},\\
\end{split}

(3.24)

where
\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-3})
is a function on
B_{\varepsilon}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
such that for all
k\geqslant 0
and
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\to\infty
,

\left|\partial^{k}\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-3})\right|=O(|\sigma||z|^{-3-k}).

(3.25)

Proof of Claim
3.12
.

The key is the following expression of
\Phi_{\sigma}
from Lemma
2.5
:

\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}z_{i}=z_{i}+\nu_{\sigma}(z)\overline{z}_{i}^{2}\;\,(i=1,2,3).

(3.26)

Hence, dropping the restriction symbols for simplicity,

\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(dz_{i})=dz_{i}+2\nu_{\sigma}(z)\overline{z}_{i}dz_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{\partial\nu_{\sigma}(z)}{\partial z_{j}}\overline{z}_{i}^{2}dz_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{\partial\nu_{\sigma}(z)}{\partial\overline{z}_{j}}\overline{z}_{i}^{2}d\overline{z}_{j}.

(3.27)

From Lemma
2.5
we have for all
\ell\geqslant 0
and for
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\to\infty
that

\left|\partial^{\ell}\nu_{\sigma}(z)\right|=O(|\sigma||z|^{-4-\ell}).

(3.28)

Therefore

\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(dz_{i})=dz_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-3})dz_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-3})d\overline{z}_{j}.

(3.29)

On
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, when
|z|\leqslant\varepsilon
for some sufficiently small constant
\varepsilon
, we have that

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{FS}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}a_{i\bar{\jmath}}(z)dz_{i}\wedge d\overline{z}_{j},\;\,a_{i\bar{\jmath}}=\delta_{i\bar{\jmath}}+O(|z|^{2})\;\,\text{as}\;\,|z|\to 0,

(3.30)

and
a_{i\bar{\jmath}}(z)
is actually real-analytic in
z
. Then, from (
3.26
) and (
3.28
),

\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}a_{i\bar{\jmath}}(z)-a_{i\bar{\jmath}}(z)=\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-1})\;\,\text{as}\;\,|\sigma|^{-\frac{1}{3}}|z|\to\infty.

(3.31)

For example, if
z_{1}^{2}
appears in
a_{i\bar{\jmath}}(z)
, then the relevant difference is

\displaystyle\begin{split}(z_{1}+\nu_{\sigma}(z)\overline{z}_{1}^{2})^{2}-z_{1}^{2}&=2\nu_{\sigma}(z)z_{1}\overline{z}_{1}^{2}+\nu_{\sigma}(z)^{2}\overline{z}_{1}^{4}\\
&=O(|\sigma||z|^{-1})+O(|\sigma|^{2}|z|^{-4})\;\,\text{as}\;\,|\sigma|^{-\frac{1}{3}}|z|\to\infty,\end{split}

(3.32)

which is
O(|\sigma||z|^{-1})
because
|\sigma||z|^{-3}=o(1)
.

Then Claim
3.12
follows from (
3.29
) and (
3.31
) because
|z|^{-1}\leqslant\varepsilon^{2}|z|^{-3}
.
∎

Next, we estimate the
2
-form (
3.24
) with respect to the metric
\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}
. To do so, without loss of generality (using the symmetry of
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
) we may assume that
|z_{3}|\geqslant\max\{|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\}
. In particular, we are working on the affine piece
z_{3}\neq 0
. From the defining equation of
TY_{0}
,

dz_{3}=-\frac{z_{1}^{2}}{z_{3}^{2}}dz_{1}-\frac{z_{2}^{2}}{z_{3}^{2}}dz_{2},

(3.33)

where we are again dropping the restriction symbols. Thus,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}=&\left(1+\left|\frac{z_{1}}{z_{3}}\right|^{4}\right)dz_{1}\wedge d\overline{z}_{1}+\left(1+\left|\frac{z_{2}}{z_{3}}\right|^{4}\right)dz_{2}\wedge d\overline{z}_{2}\\
&+\left(\frac{z_{1}^{2}}{z_{3}^{2}}\frac{\overline{z}_{2}^{2}}{\overline{z}_{3}^{2}}\right)dz_{1}\wedge d\overline{z}_{2}+\left(\frac{z_{2}^{2}}{z_{3}^{2}}\frac{\overline{z}_{1}^{2}}{\overline{z}_{3}^{2}}\right)dz_{2}\wedge d\overline{z}_{1}.\end{split}

(3.34)

Viewing
z_{1},z_{2}
as local coordinates and denoting the coefficients of the Kähler form in (
3.34
) by
g_{i\bar{\jmath}}
, it is easy to see that
\det(g_{i\bar{\jmath}})\geqslant 1
, so every component of the inverse matrix
g^{\bar{\imath}j}
is bounded by
2
because
|z_{3}|\geqslant\max\{|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\}
. This fact and (
3.24
) imply the desired estimate (
3.22
) for
k=0
.

To finish the proof, we also need to estimate the first covariant derivative of (
3.24
) with respect to
\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}
. Applying
-z_{3}^{3}=z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}
and
|z_{3}|\geqslant\max\{|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\}
, we have that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial z_{3}}{\partial z_{1}}=-\frac{z_{1}^{2}}{z_{3}^{2}}=O(1),\;\,\frac{\partial z_{3}}{\partial z_{2}}=-\frac{z_{2}^{2}}{z_{3}^{2}}=O(1).\end{split}

(3.35)

Combining the claimed estimates (
3.25
) and (
3.35
), it follows from the chain rule that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|\frac{\partial\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-3})}{\partial z_{i}}\right|+\left|\frac{\partial\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-3})}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\right|=O(|\sigma||z|^{-4})\;\,(i=1,2).\end{split}

(3.36)

The remaining task is to bound the first covariant derivative of
dz_{1},dz_{2},dz_{3}
with respect to
\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}
. Since
g^{\bar{\imath}j}
is bounded, it is enough to bound the second partials of
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
and the first partials of
g_{i\bar{\jmath}}
. This can be done by differentiating (
3.35
) and (
3.34
), respectively. Because of homogeneity reasons it is clear that all of these derivatives are bounded by a constant times
|z|^{-1}
.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

We work on
TY_{0}
and abuse notation by replacing

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\omega_{KE,0}\leadsto\omega_{KE,0},\;\;(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{0}\leadsto\psi_{0},\;\;(\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}\leadsto\psi_{FS,\sigma}.\end{split}

(3.37)

Let
N>0
be a fixed large constant.
Then for
k=0,1
and
t\in[2\tau,-N]
we have that

\left|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{KE,0}}\frac{(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}((\Phi^{-1}_{\sigma})^{*}\psi_{0}+\psi_{FS,\sigma}))^{2}-\omega^{2}_{KE,0}}{\omega^{2}_{KE,0}}\right|_{\omega_{KE,0}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.38)

Proof.

Inserting one more term, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}(i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}((\Phi^{-1}_{\sigma})^{*}\psi_{0}+\psi_{FS,\sigma}))^{2}-\omega^{2}_{KE,0}=(i\partial\overline{\partial}(\psi_{0}+\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}))^{2}-\omega^{2}_{KE,0}\\
+\;\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}(i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}((\Phi^{-1}_{\sigma})^{*}\psi_{0}+\psi_{FS,\sigma}))^{2}-(i\partial\overline{\partial}(\psi_{0}+\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}))^{2}.\end{split}

(3.39)

We begin by estimating the first line of (
3.39
). Canceling
i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{0}
, one has

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}(\psi_{0}+\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma})-\omega_{KE,0}=i\partial\overline{\partial}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0}).

(3.40)

For 2-forms
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
, one has

\displaystyle\omega_{1}^{2}-\omega_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=2(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})\wedge\omega_{2}+(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})^{2},

(3.41)

which implies that

\frac{(i\partial\overline{\partial}(\psi_{0}+\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}))^{2}-\omega^{2}_{KE,0}}{\omega^{2}_{KE,0}}=2\Delta_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0})+\frac{(i\partial\overline{\partial}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0}))^{2}}{\omega^{2}_{KE,0}}.

(3.42)

To estimate these terms, we replace the reference metric
\omega_{KE,0}
by
\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}
.

Claim 3.14
.

For any
j\geqslant 0
there is a positive integer
K_{j}
such that as
|z|\to 0
,

|\nabla^{j}_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}})|_{\omega_{KE,0}}=O(|z|^{2}|t|^{K_{j}}).

(3.43)

Moreover, for
j=0
the left-hand side is actually uniformly equivalent to
|z|^{2}|t|^{K_{0}}
.

Proof of Claim
3.14
.

Thanks to
[
10
, Thm 1.4]
,
\omega_{cusp}
and
\omega_{KE,0}
are uniformly equivalent to any order for
t\leqslant-N
, so it suffices to prove the estimate using
\omega_{cusp}
as the reference metric.

On
TY_{0}\setminus\{0\}
we clearly have that
|z|^{2}=e^{t+\psi}
for some smooth function
\psi
on the elliptic curve
E
, viewed as a
0
-homogeneous function on
TY_{0}\setminus\{0\}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}=i\partial\overline{\partial}e^{t+\psi}

\displaystyle=e^{t+\psi}i\partial\overline{\partial}(t+\psi)+e^{t+\psi}i\partial(t+\psi)\wedge\overline{\partial}(t+\psi).

(3.44)

For any fixed
t_{*}\leqslant-N
, quasi-coordinates for
\omega_{cusp}
on a neighborhood of the hypersurface
\{t=t_{*}\}
are given by
(\rho,\check{x},\check{y},\check{\theta})
, where
\rho=\log|t|
,
(\check{x},\check{y})=|t_{*}|^{-1/2}(x,y)
for a fixed pair
(x,y)
of linear coordinates on
E
and
\check{\theta}=|t_{*}|^{-1}\theta
for a fixed angular coordinate
\theta
along the Hopf circles in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\begin{split}i\partial\overline{\partial}t=|t_{*}|\cdot(\textit{a fixed $2$-form in $\check{x},\check{y}$}),\;\,\partial t=-\frac{1}{2}e^{\rho}d\rho+|t_{*}|\cdot(\textit{a fixed $1$-form in $\check{x},\check{y},\check{\theta}$}).\end{split}

(3.45)

Because covariant derivatives with respect to
\omega_{cusp}
are equivalent to ordinary partial derivatives with respect to
(\rho,\check{x},\check{y},\check{\theta})
, the claim follows from (
3.44
) and (
3.45
).
∎

We now estimate the terms on the right-hand side of (
3.42
). By Lemma
3.11
and Claim
3.14
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\Delta_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0})|\\
&\leqslant|i\partial\overline{\partial}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0})|_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}\cdot{\rm{tr}}_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}})\\
&=O(|\sigma||z|^{-3}|z|^{2}|t|^{K_{0}})\end{split}

(3.46)

as
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\sim e^{(1/2)(t-T)}\to\infty
. Because the latter condition is satisfied for
t\in[2\tau,-N]
, the claimed estimate (
3.38
) follows for the Laplacian term for
k=0
(with a good factor of
|\sigma|^{2/3}
to spare). The first derivative of this term can be estimated in a similar fashion:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\nabla_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\Delta_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0}))|_{\omega_{KE,0}}\\
&\leqslant|\nabla_{\omega_{KE,0}}(i\partial\overline{\partial}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0}))|_{\omega_{KE,0}}\\
&\leqslant|\nabla_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}(i\partial\overline{\partial}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0}))|_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}\cdot({\rm{tr}}_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}))^{\frac{3}{2}}\\
&+|(i\partial\overline{\partial}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0}))|_{\omega_{KE,0}}\cdot{\rm{tr}}_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}(\omega_{KE,0})\cdot|\nabla_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}})|_{\omega_{KE,0}}\\
&=O(|\sigma||z|^{-4}(|z|^{2}|t|^{K_{0}})^{\frac{3}{2}}+|\sigma||z|^{-3}|z|^{-2}|t|^{-K_{0}}|z|^{2}|t|^{K_{1}})\end{split}

(3.47)

as
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\to\infty
. This is again of the desired order with a factor of
|\sigma|^{2/3}
to spare. The estimate of the quadratic term and its first derivative is very similar and we omit it.

It remains to estimate the second line of (
3.39
). Using (
3.20
) with
f=(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{0}+\psi_{FS,\sigma}
and with reference metric
\omega=\omega_{KE,0}
, and using Lemma
3.8
and
[
10
, Thm 1.4]
(to compare
\omega_{cusp}
to
\omega_{KE,0}
),

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{1}|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}f-i\partial\overline{\partial}\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f)|_{\omega_{KE,0}}\leqslant O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})\sum_{k=1}^{3}|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{cusp}}\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f|_{\omega_{cusp}}.

(3.48)

We will first show that the three derivatives of
\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f
that appear on the right-hand side are
O(1)
.

To this end, observe that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f=(\psi_{FS,0}+\psi_{0})+(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0}).

(3.49)

The first term is equal to the potential
\psi_{KE,0}
of
\omega_{KE,0}
. Using Proposition
2.14
and quasi-coordinates for
\omega_{cusp}
, one checks that the
\omega_{cusp}
-gradient and all higher covariant derivatives of this potential are
O(1)
. For the second term in (
3.49
), we first show as in the proof of (
3.31
) that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0}=\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-1})|_{TY_{0}}

(3.50)

as
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\to\infty
, using the fact that
\psi_{FS}(z)
is real-analytic and
O(|z|^{2})
as
|z|\to 0
. Second, as in (
3.33
)–(
3.36
), we use (
3.50
) to further estimate the covariant derivatives of this function with respect to
\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}
. Here we also need the second covariant derivative of
dz_{i}|_{TY_{0}}
(
i=1,2,3
), but this is seen to be bounded by a constant times
|z|^{-2}
by the same argument as after (
3.36
). Thus,

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0})|_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}=O(|\sigma||z|^{-1-k})

(3.51)

for
k=1,2,3
and
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\to\infty
. Lastly, by using (
3.43
) and following the pattern of (
3.46
)–(
3.47
) (i.e., expanding
\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}u
in
\omega_{cusp}
-normal coordinates and solving for
\partial^{k}u=\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{k}u
), we get

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{cusp}}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0})|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O(|\sigma||z|^{-1}|t|^{K})=o(1)

(3.52)

for
k=1,2,3
and
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\to\infty
, where
K>0
. To summarize, the three derivatives of
\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f
on the right-hand side of (
3.48
) are
O(1)
, and this is sharp due to the contribution of
\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}\psi_{KE,0}
.

To finish the proof of the lemma, we estimate the second line of (
3.39
) using (
3.48
). To do so, let us write the second line of (
3.39
) as
\omega_{1}^{2}-\omega_{2}^{2}
. Then, taking norms with respect to
\omega_{KE,0}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left|\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}-\omega_{2}^{2}}{\omega_{KE,0}^{2}}\right|=|\omega_{1}^{2}-\omega_{2}^{2}|\leqslant|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|\cdot(2|\omega_{2}|+|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|).\end{split}

(3.53)

We estimated
|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|
in (
3.48
), and
|\omega_{2}|=O(1)
because
\omega_{2}=\omega_{KE,0}+i\partial\overline{\partial}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0})
and the
\omega_{KE,0}
-norm of the second term was proved to be
o(1)
in (
3.46
). The first derivative of the second line of (
3.39
) can be bounded in a similar fashion, using also (
3.48
) with
k=1
and (
3.47
).
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

Abuse notation by replacing
(\Psi_{0}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{0}\leadsto\psi_{0}
and
(\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}\leadsto\psi_{FS,\sigma}
.
Then:

(1)

For all
k\geqslant 0
and
t\in[2\tau,\tau]
, one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{cusp}}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{cusp}-\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{T})|_{\omega_{cusp}}\\
&=O(|b||\tau|^{3})+O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).\end{split}

(3.54)

(2)

For
k=0,1
and
t\in[2\tau,\tau],
one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{cusp}}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{cusp}-\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}((\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{0}+\psi_{FS,\sigma}))|_{\omega_{cusp}}\\
&=O(e^{-\delta_{0}\sqrt{-t}}).\end{split}

(3.55)

(3)

For
k=0,1
and
t\in[T+T_{0},T+2T_{0}]
,
one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\nabla^{k}_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{T}-|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\omega_{TY,\sigma})|_{\omega_{cusp}}\\
&=O(e^{-\delta_{0}(t-T)^{1/2}})+O(|b|^{\frac{2-k}{4}}T_{0}^{\frac{3(2-k)}{4}}).\end{split}

(3.56)

Proof.

For item
(1)
: By Lemma
2.24
, one has
for any
k\geqslant 0
and for
t\in[2\tau,\tau]
that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{k}(\omega_{T}-\omega_{cusp})|_{\omega_{cusp}}\leqslant C_{k}|b||\tau|^{3}.

(3.57)

Here
C_{k}
does not depend on
b,\tau
. Then by the triangle inequality and Lemma
3.10
,

|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{cusp}}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{cusp}-\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{T})|_{\omega_{cusp}}\leqslant C_{k}(|b||\tau|^{3}+e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.58)

For item
(2)
: By Lemma
3.10
, we can replace
\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{cusp}
on the left-hand side of the claim by
\omega_{cusp}
up to an error of
O(e^{-(1/2-\varepsilon)(t-T)})
, which is negligible because
(t-T)/|t|\rightarrow\infty
for
t\in[2\tau,\tau]
. Similarly, by Lemma
3.11
and (
3.43
) we can replace
\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{FS,\sigma}
by
\omega_{FS,0}
(note that a closely related implication was already proved in (
3.46
)–(
3.47
)). Next,

\displaystyle\omega_{cusp}-\omega_{FS,0}=(\omega_{cusp}-\omega_{KE,0})+i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{0},

(3.59)

and the first term here was estimated in Proposition
2.14
. Thus, it remains to estimate

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\omega_{cusp}}^{k}(i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{0}-\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}((\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{0}))|_{\omega_{cusp}}

(3.60)

for
k=0,1
. To do so, we use (
3.20
) with
\omega=\omega_{cusp}
and
f=(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{0}
. For this we require a bound on the first three
\omega_{cusp}
-derivatives of
\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f=\psi_{0}=\psi_{KE,0}-\psi_{FS,0}
. For
\psi_{KE,0}
, such a bound, which is actually
O(1)
, follows from Proposition
2.14
using quasi-coordinates for
\omega_{cusp}
. For
\psi_{FS,0}
, we need to go through the same steps as after (
3.58
): for
k=1,2,3
and
|z|\to 0
we clearly have that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}^{k}\psi_{FS,0}|_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}=O(|z|^{2-k}),

(3.61)

and hence, by (
3.43
) and by computations in
\omega_{cusp}
-normal coordinates, for some
K>0
,

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{cusp}}\psi_{FS,0}|_{\omega_{cusp}}=O(|z|^{2}|t|^{K})=o(1).

(3.62)

In sum, all errors are negligible compared to the one from
\omega_{cusp}-\omega_{KE,0}
, which is
O(e^{-\delta_{0}\sqrt{-t}})
.

For item
(3)
: Using (
2.76
), (
2.197
) and Lemma
3.10
, one has
for
k=0,1
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left|\nabla^{k}_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{T}-|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\omega_{TY_{\sigma}})\right|_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}\\
&=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})+O(|b|^{\frac{2-k}{4}}T_{0}^{\frac{3(2-k)}{4}})+O(e^{-\delta_{0}(t-T)^{1/2}}).\end{split}

(3.63)

When applying (
2.76
), we absorb the factor
|b|^{(2-k)/4}
into
e^{-\delta_{0}(t-T)^{1/2}}
by slightly changing
\delta_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Abuse notation by replacing
(\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\Omega_{\sigma}\leadsto\Omega_{\sigma}
. Then for some
K>0
and for all
\varepsilon>0
, for
T+T_{0}\leqslant t\leqslant-N
, for
j=0,1
and for
\omega
equal to either
\omega_{cusp}
or
\omega_{T}
, it holds on
TY_{0}
that

\left|\nabla^{j}_{\omega}\log\frac{\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}\right|_{\omega}=O(|\sigma||z|^{-3}|t|^{K})=O(e^{-(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.64)

Proof.

In the following,
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
and
dz_{1},dz_{2},dz_{3}
are automatically understood to be restricted from
\mathbb{C}^{3}
to
TY_{\sigma}
or
TY_{0}
. Similarly,
\tilde{O}(\ldots)
as in (
3.25
) denotes a function on a neighborhood of the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, and we will restrict this function to
TY_{\sigma}
or
TY_{0}
without writing the restriction symbol.

By the proof of Lemma
2.19
we have for all
\sigma
(including
\sigma=0
) that

\Omega_{\sigma}=(1+f(z))\Omega_{TY_{\sigma}},

(3.65)

where
f(z)
is holomorphic on some neighborhood of the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
with
f(0)=0
. We may assume without loss of generality that
|z_{3}|\geqslant\max\{|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\}
at the point of
TY_{0}
at which we are working. Then
z_{3}\neq 0
because
t>-\infty
. Also,
\Phi_{\sigma}(z)_{3}=z_{3}+\nu_{\sigma}(z)\overline{z}_{3}^{2}\neq 0
because otherwise
|z_{3}|=O(|\sigma||z|^{-4})|z_{3}|^{2}
, so
1=O(|\sigma||z|^{-4})|z_{3}|=O(|\sigma||z|^{-3})=o(1)
because
t-T\to\infty
, and this is a contradiction. This means that we can use the same Poincaré residue to represent
\Omega_{TY_{0}}|_{z}
and
\Omega_{TY_{\sigma}}|_{\Phi_{\sigma}(z)}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Omega_{TY_{0}}|_{z}=\frac{dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}}{z_{3}^{2}},\;\,\Omega_{TY_{\sigma}}|_{\Phi_{\sigma}(z)}=\frac{dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}}{\Phi_{\sigma}(z)_{3}^{2}}.

(3.66)

Then from (
3.26
) and (
3.29
) we have that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(z_{i})&=z_{i}+\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-2}),\\
\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(dz_{1})\wedge\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(dz_{2})&=dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}+\sum\nolimits_{|I|+|J|=2}\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-3})\,dz_{I}\wedge d\overline{z}_{J},\end{split}

(3.67)

where
I,J
are multi-indices with values in
\{1,2,3\}
. Combining (
3.65
), (
3.66
) and (
3.67
) with

\displaystyle dz_{3}=-\frac{z_{1}^{2}}{z_{3}^{2}}dz_{1}-\frac{z_{2}^{2}}{z_{3}^{2}}dz_{2},

(3.68)

which holds identically on
TY_{0}\cap\{z_{3}\neq 0\}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}&=\left|\frac{1+f(\Phi_{\sigma}(z))}{1+f(z)}\right|^{2}\cdot\frac{\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(dz_{1})\wedge\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(dz_{2})\wedge\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(d\overline{z}_{1})\wedge\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(d\overline{z}_{2})}{dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}\wedge d\overline{z}_{1}\wedge d\overline{z}_{2}}\cdot\left|\frac{z_{3}}{\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(z_{3})}\right|^{4}\\
&=\left|\frac{1+f(\Phi_{\sigma}(z))}{1+f(z)}\right|^{2}\cdot\left(1+{\rm Re}\left(\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-3})\hskip-1.42262pt\left[\frac{z_{1}}{z_{3}},\frac{z_{2}}{z_{3}}\right]\right)\right)\cdot\left|1+z_{3}^{-1}\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-2})\right|^{-4}.\end{split}

(3.69)

The square bracket notation stands for a polynomial in
z_{1}/z_{3},z_{2}/z_{3}
with
\tilde{O}(|\sigma||z|^{-3})
coefficients.

We can analyze
|\nabla^{j}_{\omega}\log\eqref{eq:tod}|_{\omega}
using the same technique as in the proofs of Lemmas
3.11
and
3.13
: First, view
z_{1},z_{2}
as local coordinates on
TY_{0}\cap\{z_{3}\neq 0\}
, represent
\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}
by a matrix and estimate the
\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}
-covariant derivatives of
\log\eqref{eq:tod}
in terms of its partial derivatives with respect to
z_{1},z_{2}
. Here, the properties
-z_{3}^{3}=z_{1}^{3}+z_{2}^{3}
and
|z_{3}|\geqslant\max\{|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\}
need to be used to eliminate or estimate terms that explicitly depend on
z_{3}
. The upshot is that for
j=0,1
and
|\sigma|^{-1/3}|z|\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\left|\nabla^{j}_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}\log\frac{\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}\right|_{\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}}=O(|\sigma||z|^{-3-j}).

(3.70)

Secondly, we can rewrite (
3.70
) in terms of
\omega_{cusp}
instead of
\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}
by using (
3.43
). This implies the statement of the lemma for
\omega=\omega_{cusp}
. For
\omega=\omega_{T}
we use a reduction as in the proof of Lemma
3.8
. Fix a small
\delta>0
. If
t/T\leqslant 1-\delta
, then
\omega_{T}
and
\omega_{cusp}
are uniformly comparable by Proposition
2.26
. Thus, the version of the lemma for
\omega_{T}
follows from the one for
\omega_{cusp}
in this case. If
t/T\geqslant 1-\delta
, then
\omega_{T}
is comparable to
|b|^{1/2}\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}
by (
2.164
)–(
2.165
). Redoing the proof of (
3.43
) for
\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}
instead of
\omega_{cusp}
as the reference metric, it is clear that
|\omega_{flat}|_{TY_{0}}|_{\omega_{\mathcal{C},\sigma}}=O(|z|^{2}|b|^{-L}(t-T)^{L})
for some
L>0
if
|z|\to 0
with
t-T\geqslant 1
. Thus, the statement of the lemma again follows from (
3.70
).
∎

We now finally come to the proof of our main result, Theorem
3.5
.

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

Firstly, let us recall from (
3.8
) the expression of the Ricci potential:

f_{\sigma}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma}}=\log\left(\frac{\omega_{glue,\sigma}^{2}}{\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}\right)-\psi_{glue,\sigma}.

(3.71)

We will do the proof case by case by analyzing this formula.

Region
\mathfrak{R}_{1}
:
In this region, we have that
t>-N
. It follows from the smoothness of
\Omega_{\sigma}
and
\psi_{FS,\sigma}
with respect to
\sigma
and from the Kähler-Einstein equation

\log\frac{\omega_{KE,0}^{2}}{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}=\psi_{KE,0}

(3.72)

that
|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}f_{\sigma}|_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}=O(|\sigma|)
for
k=0,1
.

Region
\mathfrak{R}_{2}
:
For region
\mathfrak{R}_{2}
, as in Lemma
3.13
, we omit the pull-back map
\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}
for simplicity. In this region,
{\tau}<t<-N
. Using (
3.72
), we have that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}f_{\sigma}&=\log\frac{\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}(i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{glue,\sigma})^{2}}{\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}+\log\frac{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}{\omega^{2}_{KE,0}}-\psi_{0}-\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}+\psi_{KE,0}\\
&=\log\frac{\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}(i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{glue,\sigma})^{2}}{\omega_{KE,0}^{2}}+\log\frac{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}{\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}-\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}+\psi_{FS,0}.\end{split}

(3.73)

We estimate the three terms separately.
By Lemma
3.13
, for
k=0,1
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{KE,0}}\log\frac{\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}(i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{glue,\sigma})^{2}}{\omega_{KE,0}^{2}}\right|_{\omega_{KE,0}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.74)

By Lemma
3.16
, for
k=0,1
, the second term can be estimated as

\left|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{KE,0}}\log\frac{\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}\right|_{\omega_{KE,0}}=O(e^{-(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.75)

For the third term, for
k=0,1
, by (
3.58
) and Proposition
2.14
,

|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{FS,\sigma}-\psi_{FS,0})|_{\omega_{KE,0}}=O(|\sigma||z|^{-1}|t|^{K})=O(|z|^{2}e^{-(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.76)

Dropping the smaller terms, we get

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\omega_{KE,0}}^{k}\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f_{\sigma}|_{\omega_{KE,0}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})\;\,(k=0,1).

(3.77)

Using the metric equivalence of
\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}g_{glue,\sigma}
and
g_{KE,0}
in this region, which follows from Lemmas
3.8
and
3.10
(using also (
3.52
) to compare the term
\psi_{FS,\sigma}
in
\psi_{glue,\sigma}
to
\psi_{FS,0}
), we conclude that

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}f_{\sigma}|_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})\;\,(k=0,1).

(3.78)

Region
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
:
In this case
\psi_{glue,\sigma}=(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{T}
and
\omega_{glue,\sigma}=i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\psi_{T}
.
So we have

f_{\sigma}=\log\frac{(i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{glue,\sigma})^{2}}{\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}-(\Phi^{-1}_{\sigma})^{*}\psi_{T}.

(3.79)

Hence

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}f_{\sigma}&=\log\frac{\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}(i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{glue,\sigma})^{2}}{\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}-\psi_{T}\\
&=\log\frac{\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}(i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{glue,\sigma})^{2}}{\omega_{T}^{2}}+\log\frac{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}{\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}+\log\frac{\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}}{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}+\log\frac{\omega^{2}_{T}}{\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}}-\psi_{T}.\end{split}

(3.80)

By equation (
2.46
), the last two terms combine to zero.

Now we estimate the first three terms one by one. Using Lemma
3.10
, for
j=0,1
one has

\left|\nabla^{j}_{\omega_{T}}\left(\log\frac{\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}(i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}\psi_{glue,\sigma})^{2}}{\omega_{T}^{2}}\right)\right|_{\omega_{T}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.81)

By Lemma
3.16
, for
j=0,1,
we estimate the second term by

\left|\nabla^{j}_{\omega_{T}}\left(\log\frac{\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}\right)\right|_{\omega_{T}}=O(e^{-(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.82)

By (
2.49
) and (
2.50
) with
C=1
, for
j=0,1
we estimate the third term by

\left|\nabla^{j}_{\omega_{T}}\left(\log\frac{\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}}{\Omega_{0}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{0}}\right)\right|_{\omega_{T}}=O(e^{\frac{(1-\varepsilon)t}{2}}).

(3.83)

Dropping the smaller term, one has

|\nabla_{\omega_{T}}^{j}\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}f_{\sigma}|_{\omega_{T}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})+O(e^{(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)t})\;\,(j=0,1).

(3.84)

Using the metric equivalence of
\Phi^{*}_{\sigma}g_{glue,\sigma}
and
g_{T}
in this region, we conclude that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}^{j}f_{\sigma}|_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}=O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})+O(e^{(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)t})\;\,(j=0,1).

(3.85)

Regions
\mathfrak{R}_{6},\mathfrak{R}_{7}
:
For regions
\mathfrak{R}_{6},\mathfrak{R}_{7}
we estimate
f_{\sigma}
directly on
TY_{\sigma}
. In these two cases, one has

f_{\sigma}=\log\frac{(\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}m_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{TY_{1}})^{2}}{\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}-\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}m_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{TY_{1}}-\psi_{T}(T).

(3.86)

It follows from

{(m_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{TY_{1}})^{2}}={m_{\sigma}^{*}\Omega_{TY_{1}}\wedge m_{\sigma}^{*}\overline{\Omega}_{TY_{1}}}

(3.87)

that

\log\frac{(\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}m_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{TY_{1}})^{2}}{\Omega_{\sigma}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\sigma}}-\psi_{T}(T)=\log\Big(\mathfrak{c}^{2}|b||F_{\sigma}|^{2}e^{-\psi_{T}(T)}\Big),

(3.88)

where

\displaystyle F_{\sigma}:=\frac{m_{\sigma}^{*}\Omega_{TY_{1}}}{\Omega_{\sigma}}.

(3.89)

Now we claim that

\mathfrak{c}^{2}|b|=e^{\psi_{T}(T)}.

(3.90)

This can easily be checked as follows: Using the fact that
\mathfrak{c}=\sqrt{2}
in dimension
n=2
and that

e^{\psi_{T}(T)+a}+b=0,

(3.91)

we see that (
3.90
) is equivalent to
a=-{\log 2}
, which follows from the equation

e^{-\psi_{cusp}}\omega_{cusp}^{2}=\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{C}}=\omega_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}

(3.92)

and from the explicit form of
\psi_{cusp},\psi_{\mathcal{C}}
in (
2.45
), (
2.177
) respectively.

Hence

\log\Big(\mathfrak{c}^{2}|b||F_{\sigma}|^{2}e^{-\psi_{T}(T)}\Big)=\log|F_{\sigma}|^{2}.

(3.93)

On
TY_{1}
, if
|z|\gg 1
, then
|z|^{2}
is uniformly comparable to
h=e^{t}
.
Recall that
m_{\sigma}(z)=\sigma^{-1/3}z
. So on
TY_{\sigma}
, points in regions
\mathfrak{R}_{6}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{7}
satisfy, for some constant
C
independent of
\sigma
,

\displaystyle|z|^{2}\leqslant C|\sigma|^{\frac{2}{3}}e^{T_{0}}=Ce^{T+T_{0}}.

(3.94)

Using Lemma
2.19
, one has

\log|F_{\sigma}|=\log\left|\frac{m_{\sigma}^{*}\Omega_{TY_{1}}}{\Omega_{\sigma}}\right|=\tilde{O}(|z|)=O(e^{\frac{T+T_{0}}{2}}).

(3.95)

Noticing that
\mathfrak{c}|b|^{1/2}m_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{TY_{1}}
is Ricci-flat, we can deduce from the Cheng-Yau gradient estimate for harmonic functions
[
7
, p.350, Thm 6]
and from (
3.95
) that

\left|\nabla_{\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}m_{\sigma}^{*}\omega_{TY_{1}}}(\log|F_{\sigma}|^{2})\right|_{\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}m_{\sigma}^{*}\omega_{TY_{1}}}=O(|b|^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{\frac{T+T_{0}}{2}}).

(3.96)

Using that
|b|
is comparable to
|T|^{-3}
, we have

\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}m_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{TY_{1}}=O((T_{0}/|T|)^{\frac{3}{2}}).

(3.97)

Also, for
\log h\leqslant T_{0}
,

\left|\nabla_{\omega_{TY_{1}}}\psi_{TY_{1}}\right|_{\omega_{TY_{1}}}=O(T_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}),

(3.98)

and hence

\left|\nabla_{\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}m_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{TY_{1}}}\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}m_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{TY_{1}}\right|_{\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}m_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{TY_{1}}}=O(|b|^{\frac{1}{4}}T_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}).

(3.99)

Thus, combining the
C^{0}
norm and the
C^{1}
seminorm (weighted by
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}
) and dropping the smaller terms (
3.95
)–(
3.96
), we finish the proof of the case of regions
\mathfrak{R}_{6}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{7}
.

Region
\mathfrak{R}_{3}
:
In this region, we glue the two Kähler-Einstein metrics
\omega_{KE,0}
and
\omega_{T}
. Set
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
to be the expression of

\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{glue,\sigma}
in regions
\mathfrak{R}_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{4}
respectively. By (
3.73
) and (
3.75
),

\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f_{\sigma}=\log\frac{\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}(i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}((\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}(\psi_{2}+\chi_{2}(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}))))^{2}}{\omega_{KE,0}^{2}}-(\psi_{2}+\chi_{2}(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}))+O(e^{-(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)}).

(3.100)

If the error term
\chi_{2}(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2})
vanishes, this is exactly the case of region
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
, see (
3.85
).
In general, we first apply (
3.20
) to change

\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}i\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\partial}_{\sigma}f
to
i\partial\overline{\partial}\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}f
for
f=(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}(\psi_{2}+\chi_{2}(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}))
.
Then we only need to estimate the following additional terms:

\displaystyle\frac{2i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi_{2}\wedge i\partial\overline{\partial}(\chi_{2}(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}))+(i\partial\overline{\partial}(\chi_{2}(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2})))^{2}}{\omega_{KE,0}^{2}}-\chi_{2}(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}).

(3.101)

As
t\in[2\tau,\tau],
we have, for all
j\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle|\chi_{2}^{(j)}(t)|\leqslant C_{j}|\tau|^{-j}.

(3.102)

Applying the quasi-coordinates (
2.109
), we obtain that

\displaystyle|\nabla^{j}_{\omega_{KE,0}}\chi_{2}|\leqslant C_{j}.

(3.103)

Combining (
3.103
) with the estimate of
\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}
from Proposition
2.14
and (
2.111
), we deduce that

\displaystyle|\nabla^{j}_{\omega_{KE,0}}(\chi_{2}(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}))|\leqslant C_{j}(|b||\tau|^{3}+e^{-\delta_{0}\sqrt{-t}})\;\,(j=0,1).

(3.104)

Thus, since
g_{KE,0}
and
\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}g_{glue,\sigma}
are uniformly equivalent for
t\in[2\tau,\tau]
,

\displaystyle|\nabla^{j}_{\Phi_{\sigma}^{*}g_{glue,\sigma}}(\chi_{2}(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}))|\leqslant C_{j}(|b||\tau|^{3}+e^{-\delta_{0}\sqrt{-t}})\;\,(j=0,1).

(3.105)

This provides the required estimate of the cutoff errors (
3.101
).

Region
\mathfrak{R}_{5}
:
The situation is similar to
\mathfrak{R}_{3}
. Here we glue
\mathfrak{c}|b|^{1/2}m_{\sigma}^{*}\omega_{TY_{1}}
and
\omega_{T}
. From the estimate (
3.97
) in region
\mathfrak{R}_{6}
and elementary inequalities, we deduce that

|f_{\sigma}|+\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}|\nabla_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}f_{\sigma}|_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}\leqslant C(T_{0}/|T|)^{\frac{3}{2}}+|i\partial\overline{\partial}(\chi_{1}E)|_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C}}}+\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}\left|\nabla_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C}}}i\partial\overline{\partial}(\chi_{1}E)\right|_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C}}},

(3.106)

where
E(t):=\psi_{T}(t)-\psi_{T}(T)-\mathfrak{c}|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi_{\mathcal{C}}(t)
is the difference of Kähler potentials defined in (
2.179
). We estimate
\chi_{1}E
as follows, using the same idea as in Proposition
2.34
. Firstly, for
t-T\in[T_{0},2T_{0}]
,

\displaystyle|\chi_{1}^{(j)}(t)|\leqslant C_{j}T_{0}^{-j}\;\,(j\geqslant 0).

(3.107)

Using the estimate of
E
from (
2.198
), we further deduce that there exists a
C
such that, for
t-T\in[T_{0},2T_{0}]
and
j=0,1,2,3
,

\displaystyle|((t-T)\partial_{t})^{j}(\chi_{1}E)|\leqslant C|b|T_{0}^{3}.

(3.108)

Using quasi-coordinates, we have,
for
t-T\in[T_{0},2T_{0}]
and
j=0,1
,

\left|\nabla^{j}_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C}}}(i\partial\overline{\partial}(\chi_{1}E))\right|_{|b|^{\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{C}}}\leqslant C|b|^{\frac{1}{4}(2-j)}T_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}(2-j)}.

(3.109)

The claimed estimate in region
\mathfrak{R}_{5}
now follows from (
3.106
)
and (
3.109
).

This completes the proof of Theorem
3.5
.
∎

3.3.
Definition of the weight functions and weighted Hölder norms

We have set up the pre-glued manifold
(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma},\omega_{glue,\sigma})
and the relevant Monge-Ampère equation in (
3.4
)–(
3.6
) and estimated its right-hand side, i.e., the Ricci potential
f_{\sigma}
of
\omega_{glue,\sigma}
, in Theorem
3.5
. In Remark
3.7
we discussed what can be said about the solution
u_{\sigma}
using off-the-shelf arguments. This was insufficient (only) on the Tian-Yau region, motivating the development of a weighted Hölder space theory.

Definition 3.17
.

Fix a parameter
\delta>0
. In the rest of the paper
\delta
will always be chosen arbitrarily close to zero. With this in mind we define two weight functions
{w}_{\sigma},\tilde{w}_{\sigma}:\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
as follows:

\displaystyle w_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}|T|^{-\delta}&\text{on}\;\,\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}(\Phi_{\sigma}(\{t>-N\}))\cup(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\setminus{\rm dom}\,\Psi_{\sigma}),\\
\Psi_{\sigma}^{*}(\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1})^{*}\left((t-T)^{-\delta}\right)&\text{on}\;\,\Psi_{\sigma}^{-1}(\Phi_{\sigma}(\{t<-N\})),\\
1&\text{on}\;\,\mathfrak{R}_{7},\end{cases}

(3.110)

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}^{-2}w_{\sigma}.

(3.111)

Definition 3.18
.

With the weight
w_{\sigma}
from (
3.110
) and the regularity scale
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}
from (
3.9
) we define for all
0\leqslant k\leqslant 4
and for all locally
C^{k}
functions
\phi
on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
:

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{C^{k}_{w}}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left\|w_{\sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}^{j}\left|\nabla^{j}_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}\phi\right|_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma})}.

(3.112)

Moreover, for all
0\leqslant k\leqslant 3
and
\bar{\alpha}\in(0,1)
and for all locally
C^{k,\bar{\alpha}}
functions
\phi
on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
,

\displaystyle[\phi]_{C^{k,\bar{\alpha}}_{w}}:=\sup\left\{\frac{\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}(p)^{k+\bar{\alpha}}}{w_{\sigma}(p)}\frac{|(\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}\phi)(p)-(\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}\phi)(q)|_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}}{d_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}(p,q)^{\bar{\alpha}}}:0<d_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}(p,q)<\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}(p)\right\}.

(3.113)

Here the numerator of the difference quotient is to be understood using the trivialization of the tangent bundle in quasi-coordinates. Given this, we define

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{C^{k,\bar{\alpha}}_{{w}}}:=\|\phi\|_{C^{k}_{w}}+[\phi]_{C^{k,\bar{\alpha}}_{w}}.

(3.114)

Replacing
w_{\sigma}
by
\tilde{w}_{\sigma}
, we may similarly define a weighted
C^{k}_{\tilde{w}}
and
C^{k,\bar{\alpha}}_{\tilde{w}}
norm. For us,
\bar{\alpha}
will always be an arbitrary number in
(0,1)
whose choice affects neither the arguments nor the results.

Table
2
summarizes the behavior of the scale and weight functions and of the Ricci potential in a simplified manner (ignoring constant factors and allowing slightly suboptimal exponents). The only remaining geometric parameters are
|T|\sim|{\log|\sigma|}|
and
|\tau|\sim(\log|T|)^{2}
. This information is sufficient to understand almost all of the numerology in Sections
4
–
5
.

Table 2.

\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}
, weights
w_{\sigma},\tilde{w}_{\sigma}
, Ricci potential
f_{\sigma}
.

It is clear from standard Schauder theory on a ball of radius
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma}
in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
that for every
\bar{\alpha}\in(0,1)
there exists a constant
C(\bar{\alpha})
independent of
\sigma
such that the linearization

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}:=\Delta_{\omega_{glue,\sigma}}-{\rm Id}

(3.115)

of the complex Monge-Ampère operator satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{C^{2,\bar{\alpha}}_{w}}\leqslant C(\bar{\alpha})(\|L_{\sigma}\phi\|_{C^{0,\bar{\alpha}}_{\tilde{w}}}+\|\phi\|_{C^{0}_{w}})

(3.116)

for all functions
\phi
. It is also clear that
L_{\sigma}
is invertible and that we have a uniform
L^{2}
bound for
L_{\sigma}^{-1}
, i.e.,
\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\|L_{\sigma}\phi\|_{L^{2}}
for all
\phi
with respect to the
L^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma},\omega_{glue,\sigma})
norm on both sides. However, we will see in Section
4
that the obvious desirable strengthening,
\|\phi\|_{C^{0}_{w}}\leqslant C\|L_{\sigma}\phi\|_{C^{0}_{\tilde{w}}}
, does not hold for all
\phi
for any
C
independent of
\sigma
. This requires us to introduce an obstruction space.

4.
Uniform estimate of the inverse of the linearization modulo obstructions

In Section
4.1
we construct a
1
-dimensional function space
\mathbb{R}\cdot\hat{u}_{\sigma}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma})
such that there is a chance of proving uniform weighted Hölder estimates for the inverse of the restriction of
L_{\sigma}
to the
L^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma},\omega_{glue,\sigma})
-orthogonal complement of
\mathbb{R}\cdot\hat{u}_{\sigma}
. In the rest of this section, starting in Section
4.2
, we then prove via a standard blowup-and-contradiction scheme that these uniform estimates are actually true. The lack of uniformity on the obstruction space
\mathbb{R}\cdot\hat{u}_{\sigma}
will be dealt with in Section
5
.

4.1.
Definition of the obstruction space

We first sketch the idea:
\hat{u}_{\sigma}
is constant to the left and zero to the right of the middle neck
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
, and converges in a sufficiently strong sense to
\hat{u}
, a solution to an ODE
L_{\infty}\hat{u}=0
on the half-line
\mathbb{R}^{+}={\rm GH}\lim_{\sigma\to 0}\mathfrak{R}_{4}
with boundary values
1
on the left and
0
on the right. The solution
\hat{u}
is uniquely determined by these conditions and is precisely the obstruction that breaks the obvious attempt at proving uniform estimates for
L_{\sigma}^{-1}
via blowup and contradiction. However, going from
\hat{u}
to
\hat{u}_{\sigma}
turns out to be quite complicated because
\hat{u}_{\sigma}
is
not
uniquely defined by the properties we need it to satisfy, so there is no canonical choice of
\hat{u}_{\sigma}
and we need to come up with some construction that works. We now describe our solution to this problem.

The first step is to construct good coordinates on the neck
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
, which will be used throughout this subsection and which will allow us to state the crucial Proposition
4.2
.

As in Corollary
2.29
and Proposition
2.30
, we parametrize the model neck by

\displaystyle s=1-\frac{t}{T}\in(0,1)\;\,\text{and}\;\,\eta=1-s=\frac{t}{T}\in(0,1).

(4.1)

For any
s_{1}<s_{2}
in
(0,1)
we introduce coordinates on the universal cover of
\{s_{1}<s<s_{2}\}
via

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\check{x}_{\alpha},\check{y}_{\alpha},\check{x},\check{\theta}):=((-t_{*})^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{\alpha},(-t_{*})^{-\frac{1}{2}}y_{\alpha},-t_{*}x,(-t_{*})^{-1}\theta),\\
t_{*}:=\left(1-\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}\right)T,\;\,x:=-\frac{1}{t}.\end{split}

(4.2)

Note that, as a function on the universal cover,

\displaystyle s=s(\check{x})=1-\frac{1-\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}}{\check{x}}

(4.3)

is increasing in
\check{x}
, and is uniformly smoothly bounded in the chart (
4.2
) if
s_{1},s_{2}
are fixed.

Convention 4.1
.

From now on we identify the middle neck
\mathfrak{R}_{4}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
with
\{T+2T_{0}<t<2\tau\}\subset TY_{0}
via the diffeomorphism
\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\sigma}
. Then
s,\eta
naturally become functions on
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
and
\check{x}_{\alpha},\check{y}_{\alpha},\check{x},\check{\theta}
become functions on the universal cover of
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
. The range of
s
on
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
is an open interval which exhausts all of
(0,1)
as
\sigma\to 0
. Also, the
J_{\sigma}
-Kähler metric
\omega_{glue,\sigma}
on
\mathfrak{R}_{4}
then has the same Kähler potential,
\psi_{T}
, as the
J_{0}
-Kähler metric
\omega_{T}
. By Lemma
3.10
, the difference of the associated metric tensors
g_{glue,\sigma}
and
g_{T}
measured with respect to either of them is
O(e^{-(1/2-\varepsilon)(t-T)})
including all covariant derivatives.

In addition to defining the obstruction function
\hat{u}_{\sigma}
(which will be done in Definition
4.9
), our final goal in this subsection is to prove the following property of
\hat{u}_{\sigma}
, a key result of this paper. The main cause of complication in our definition of
\hat{u}_{\sigma}
is the need to ensure that this is true. Recall the radial volume density
\mu_{\infty}(s)
from Lemma
2.31
and the weight
w_{\sigma}
from Definition
3.17
.

Proposition 4.2
.

For
\sigma_{i}\to 0
let
x_{i}\in\mathfrak{R}_{4}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\sigma_{i}}
satisfy
s(x_{i})\to c\in(0,1)
and let
\psi_{i}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma_{i}})
satisfy
w_{\sigma_{i}}(x_{i})|\psi_{i}|\leqslant w_{\sigma_{i}}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma_{i}}
. Assume that there exists
\psi_{\infty}:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
such that for all
s_{1}<s_{2}
in
(0,1)
,
\psi_{i}\to\psi_{\infty}\circ s
uniformly in the coordinates (
4.2
) on the universal cover of
\{s_{1}<s<s_{2}\}
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}|T_{i}|^{2}\int_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma_{i}}}\psi_{i}\hat{u}_{\sigma_{i}}\,\omega_{glue,\sigma_{i}}^{2}=2\pi{\rm Vol}(E)\int_{0}^{1}\psi_{\infty}(s)\hat{u}(s)\,\mu_{\infty}(s)\,ds.

(4.4)

The remaining steps are now roughly as follows:

•

In Lemma
4.3
we prove that as
\sigma\to 0
the operators
L_{\sigma}|_{\mathfrak{R}_{4}}
collapse to a second-order ordinary differential operator
L_{\infty}
on the interval
s\in(0,1)
. Moreover,
L_{\infty}
is asymptotic to an explicit model operator
L_{\infty}^{\pm}
near each of the two endpoints.

•

Fundamental solutions for
L_{\infty}^{\pm}
can be calculated explicitly (Lemma
4.4
).

•

These endpoint asymptotics imply a Liouville theorem (Lemma
4.5
) of independent interest: up to scalar multiples there exists a unique entire solution
\hat{u}
to
L_{\infty}\hat{u}=0
compatible with our weights
w_{\sigma}
, and we have that
\hat{u}(s)\to 1
as
s\to 0^{+}
and
\hat{u}(s)\to 0
as
s\to 1^{-}
.

•

The inhomogeneous ODE
L_{\infty}\hat{v}=\hat{u}
has a unique solution
\hat{v}
satisfying
\hat{v}(s)\to 0
as
s\to 1^{-}
and satisfying a Neumann type boundary condition as
s\to 0^{+}
(Lemma
4.8
).

•

We transplant
\hat{v}
to a function
\hat{v}_{\sigma}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
using radial cutoff functions and define
\hat{u}_{\sigma}:=L_{\sigma}\hat{v}_{\sigma}
(Definition
4.9
). These functions
\hat{u}_{\sigma}
converge back to
\hat{u}
as
\sigma\to 0
(Lemma
4.10
).

•

Using the Neumann property of
\hat{v}
, we prove that
\hat{u}_{\sigma}
also satisfies Proposition
4.2
.

We will carry out these steps in the following sub-subsections.

4.1.1.
Collapse to an ODE on an interval

The study of the limit ODE operator
L_{\infty}
requires careful attention to various fractional exponents. We work in the general
n
-dimensional setting because the values of these exponents might look like random numbers for
n=2
.

Lemma 4.3
.

There exists a smooth second-order linear differential operator
L_{\infty}
on
(0,1)
such that:

(1)

Let
\sigma_{i}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
. Let
\psi_{i}\in C^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma_{i}})
be such that for any fixed
s_{1}<s_{2}
in
(0,1)
,

\sup_{\{s_{1}<s<s_{2}\}}|\psi_{i}|=O_{s_{1},s_{2}}(1)\;\,\text{and}\;\,\sup_{\{s_{1}<s<s_{2}\}}|L_{\sigma_{i}}\psi_{i}|=o_{s_{1},s_{2}}(1)\;\,\text{as}\;\,i\to\infty.

(4.5)

Then there is a smooth function
\psi_{\infty}:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
such that
L_{\infty}\psi_{\infty}=0
and, after passing to a subsequence and pulling back to the universal cover, we have for all
s_{1}<s_{2}
that
\psi_{i}\to\psi_{\infty}\circ s
weakly in
W^{2,p}_{loc}
and strongly in
C^{1,\beta}_{loc}
with respect to the coordinates (
4.2
) for all
p
and
\beta
.

(2)

While there is no simple formula for
L_{\infty}
globally on
(0,1)
, at the endpoints we have that

\displaystyle L_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n+1}L_{\infty}^{+}+O(\eta^{n+1})\;\,\text{as}\;\,\eta=1-s\to 0^{+},

(4.6)

\displaystyle L_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d(n)}L_{\infty}^{-}+O(s^{\frac{n+1}{n}})\;\,\text{as}\;\,s\to 0^{+},\;\,d(n):=\left(\frac{n}{n+1}\right)^{\frac{1}{n}}c(n)^{\frac{n+1}{n}}.

(4.7)

Here the
O
notation is to be understood in the sense of Definition
2.27
,
c(n)
is as in (
2.90
), and the two model operators
L_{\infty}^{\pm}
are given by

\displaystyle L_{\infty}^{+}

\displaystyle:=\eta^{2}\partial_{\eta\eta}^{2}-(n-1)\eta\partial_{\eta}-(n+1)\cdot{\rm Id},

(4.8)

\displaystyle L_{\infty}^{-}

\displaystyle:=ns^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\partial^{2}_{ss}+(n-1)s^{-\frac{1}{n}}\partial_{s}-d(n)\cdot{\rm Id}.

(4.9)

Proof.

The chart
(\check{x},\check{\theta},\check{x}_{\alpha},\check{y}_{\alpha})
identifies the universal cover of
\{s_{1}<s<s_{2}\}
with

\left(\frac{1-\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}}{1-s_{1}},\frac{1-\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}}{1-s_{2}}\right)\times\mathbb{R}^{2n-1},

(4.10)

where the interval is compactly contained in
(0,1)
. As recalled in Convention
4.1
,
g_{glue,\sigma}
and
g_{T}
are uniformly smoothly bounded on this coordinate slab and differ from each other by

\displaystyle O(e^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)(t-T)})\;\,\text{with}\;\,t-T\geqslant 2|T|^{\alpha}\;\,\text{as}\;\,\sigma\to 0,

(4.11)

including all derivatives. Also, by construction,
g_{glue,\sigma_{i}}
,
g_{T_{i}}
and
\psi_{i}
are invariant under the deck group
\Gamma_{i}
of the universal cover, which preserves
\check{x}
and acts as a discrete Heisenberg group with fundamental domain diameter
\sim(-t_{*,i})^{-1/2}\sim|T_{i}|^{-1/2}\sim|{\log|\sigma_{i}|}|^{-1/2}
on the coordinates
(\check{\theta},\check{x}_{\alpha},\check{y}_{\alpha})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
.

By assumption, the
\Gamma_{i}
-invariant functions
|\psi_{i}|
are uniformly bounded and the
\Gamma_{i}
-invariant functions
|L_{\sigma_{i}}\psi_{i}|
uniformly converge to zero as
i\to\infty
. Here,
L_{\sigma_{i}}=\Delta_{\omega_{glue,\sigma_{i}}}-{\rm Id}
is a uniformly elliptic sequence of differential operators whose coefficients are uniformly smoothly bounded with respect to
i
. Thus, by standard
L^{p}
elliptic regularity theory on balls,
\psi_{i}
is locally uniformly bounded in
W^{2,p}
for every
p\in(1,\infty)
and hence, by Morrey embedding, in
C^{1,\beta}
for every
\beta\in(0,1)
. By applying the Alaoglu and Arzelà-Ascoli compactness theorems and passing to a diagonal subsequence, we have that
\psi_{i}\to\psi_{\infty}
locally weakly in
W^{2,p}
for all
p\in(1,\infty)
and strongly in
C^{1,\beta}
for all
\beta\in(0,1)
. The subsequence can be taken to be independent of
s_{1},s_{2}
by letting
s_{1}\to 0
,
s_{2}\to 1
and diagonalizing. Moreover, for any points
p,q
in our chart with
s(p)=s(q)
, we can estimate
|\psi_{i}(p)-\psi_{i}(q)|
as follows: Without loss of generality,
p,q
lie in a fundamental domain of
\Gamma_{i}
(because
\psi_{i}
is invariant under
\Gamma_{i}
) and are joined by a curve
\gamma
with
s\circ\gamma=const
whose
\omega_{glue,\sigma_{i}}
-length is
O((-t_{*,i})^{-1/2})=O(|{\log|\sigma_{i}|}|^{-1/2})\to 0
. Since the gradient of
\psi_{i}
with respect to
\omega_{glue,\sigma_{i}}
is uniformly bounded, this implies that
|\psi_{i}(p)-\psi_{i}(q)|=O(|{\log|\sigma_{i}|}|^{-1/2})
and hence
\psi_{\infty}(p)=\psi_{\infty}(q)
. Thus,
\psi_{\infty}
depends only on the radial coordinate
s
. This means that the first-order classical derivatives
\psi_{\infty,\check{x}_{\alpha}},\psi_{\infty,\check{y}_{\alpha}},\psi_{\infty,\check{\theta}}
are identically zero, so their weak gradients, which exist locally in
L^{p}
, are zero a.e. Thus, the corresponding first-order derivatives of
\psi_{i}
go to zero strongly in
C^{0,\beta}_{loc}
for every
\beta
and their gradients go to zero weakly in
L^{p}_{loc}
for every
p
.

We now argue that
\psi_{\infty}
is a weak, hence classical, solution of an ODE
L_{\infty}\psi_{\infty}=0
, where
L_{\infty}
is a smooth second-order linear differential operator in
s\in(0,1)
which is independent of the sequence
\psi_{i}
and is asymptotically modeled by
L_{\infty}^{\pm}
at the two endpoints. The key to this are Corollary
2.29
and Proposition
2.30
. Written in terms of
(\eta,\check{x}_{\alpha},\check{y}_{\alpha},\check{\theta})
, the former says that for
\eta\leqslant\delta<1
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{\omega_{T_{i}}}&=\frac{1}{n+1}(\eta^{2}\partial_{\eta\eta}^{2}-(n-1)\eta\partial_{\eta})\cdot e^{O_{\delta}(\eta^{n+1})}\\
&+\check{C}\eta^{2}\partial^{2}_{\check{\theta}\check{\theta}}+\eta\check{T}+(-t_{*,i})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\eta\check{H}_{i}+O_{\delta}(|b_{i}|^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\tau_{i}),\end{split}

(4.12)

where
\check{C}>0
is a constant,
\check{T}
is a constant coefficient Laplacian in the
\check{x}_{\alpha},\check{y}_{\alpha}
coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{2n-2}
, and
\check{H}_{i}
is a differential operator linear homogeneous in

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\check{x}_{\alpha}\check{\theta}},\partial^{2}_{\check{y}_{\alpha}\check{\theta}},(-t_{*,i})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial^{2}_{\check{\theta}\check{\theta}}

(4.13)

with coefficients that are uniformly smoothly bounded and
\Gamma_{i}
-invariant functions of
\check{x}_{\alpha},\check{y}_{\alpha}
. Applying the identity (
4.12
) to
\psi_{i}
and subtracting
\psi_{i}
, we now observe the following:

•

The left-hand side can be written as

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\omega_{T_{i}}}-{\rm Id})\psi_{i}=L_{\sigma_{i}}\psi_{i}+O(e^{-\frac{1}{2}|T_{i}|^{\alpha}})\circledast(\check{\partial}\psi_{i},\check{\partial}^{2}\psi_{i}),

(4.14)

with
\check{\partial}
the standard gradient operator in our fixed coordinate chart. The first term uniformly converges to zero by assumption. The second term goes to zero strongly in
L^{p}_{loc}
for every
p
.

•

On the right-hand side, every term with at least one tangential derivative, i.e., the second line of (
4.12
) applied to
\psi_{i}
, goes to zero weakly in
L^{p}_{loc}
for every
p
.

This shows that
L_{\infty}\psi_{\infty}=0
weakly, hence classically, with
L_{\infty}+{\rm Id}
the smooth ordinary differential operator in the first line of (
4.12
). The claim (
4.6
) is also clear from this. For the behavior of
L_{\infty}
as
s\to 0^{+}
we can run a similar argument based on Proposition
2.30
.
∎

4.1.2.
Fundamental solutions of the endpoint model operators

These are computed in Lemma
4.4
. What makes our life difficult in this paper is precisely the fact that both fundamental solutions of
L_{\infty}^{-}
are bounded as
s\to 0^{+}
: if one of them was at least
\geqslant s^{-\delta}
for some
\delta>0
, which might be one’s first guess based on experience with other singularities, then the gluing would be unobstructed.

Lemma 4.4
.

Recall that if
h_{1},h_{2}
are two fundamental solutions of a second order linear ODE, then their Wronskian is defined as
w=h_{1}h_{2}^{\prime}-h_{1}^{\prime}h_{2}
. Then the following hold.

(1)

Two fundamental solutions of
L_{\infty}^{+}
and their Wronskian are

h^{+}_{1}(\eta)=\eta^{n+1},\;\,h^{+}_{2}(\eta)=\eta^{-1},\;\,w^{+}(\eta)=-(n+2)\eta^{n-1}.

(4.15)

(2)

Denote
\lambda=(\frac{4n}{(n+1)^{2}}\cdot d(n))^{1/2}
with
d(n)
defined in (
4.7
). Recall the modified Bessel functions
I_{\frac{1}{n+1}},K_{\frac{1}{n+1}}
of order
\frac{1}{n+1}
. Write
\mathbb{R}\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}\}
to denote the ring of convergent power series in
y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
with real coefficients. Two fundamental solutions of
L_{\infty}^{-}
and their Wronskian are

\displaystyle h_{1}^{-}(s)=s^{\frac{1}{2n}}I_{\frac{1}{n+1}}(\lambda s^{\frac{n+1}{2n}})\in s^{\frac{1}{n}}\cdot\mathbb{R}\{s^{\frac{n+1}{n}}\}\subset\mathbb{R}\{s^{\frac{1}{n}},s^{\frac{n+1}{n}}\},

(4.16)

\displaystyle h_{2}^{-}(s)=s^{\frac{1}{2n}}K_{\frac{1}{n+1}}(\lambda s^{\frac{n+1}{2n}})\in\mathbb{R}\{s^{\frac{n+1}{n}}\}+s^{\frac{1}{n}}\cdot\mathbb{R}\{s^{\frac{n+1}{n}}\}\subset\mathbb{R}\{s^{\frac{1}{n}},s^{\frac{n+1}{n}}\},

(4.17)

\displaystyle w^{-}(s)=-\frac{n+1}{2n}s^{\frac{1-n}{n}}.

(4.18)

Proof.

The computations for
L_{\infty}^{+}
are straightforward. For
L_{\infty}^{-}
, a lengthy computation shows that
u(s)
solves
L_{\infty}^{-}u=0
if and only
u(s)=s^{\frac{1}{2n}}v(\lambda\cdot s^{\frac{n+1}{2n}})
, where
v(y)
solves the modified Bessel equation

y^{2}v^{\prime\prime}(y)+yv^{\prime}(y)-\left(\frac{1}{(n+1)^{2}}+y^{2}\right)v(y)=0.

(4.19)

This yields the fundamental solutions
h_{1}^{-}(s),h_{2}^{-}(s)
above. Their Wronskian is easily calculated using the fact that the Wronskian of the modified Bessel functions
I(y),K(y)
(of any order) is
-\frac{1}{y}
.
∎

4.1.3.
A Liouville theorem for the limit ODE

The ODE
L_{\infty}u=0
on the whole limit neck
(0,1)
has a
2
-dimensional vector space of solutions. Our goal is to prove that the weight
s^{-\delta}
that we will impose as an upper bound in our blowup argument singles out a
1
-dimensional subspace. Again, experience with other gluing problems suggests that by choosing
0<\delta\ll 1

all
solutions to
L_{\infty}u=0
are ruled out, but here we are left with a
1
-dimensional obstruction space no matter how small we choose
\delta
.

Lemma 4.5
.

There is a unique solution
\hat{u}(s)
to the homogeneous ODE
L_{\infty}\hat{u}=0
on
(0,1)
such that
\hat{u}(s)\to 1
as
s\to 0^{+}
and
\hat{u}(s)\to 0
as
s\to 1^{-}
. This satisfies the following properties:

(1)

\hat{u}(s)
is strictly decreasing in
s
.

(2)

As
\eta\to 0^{+}
we have that
\hat{u}(1-\eta)=O(\eta^{n+1})
.

(3)

As
s\to 0^{+}
we have that
\hat{u}(s)=1-C_{1}s^{\frac{1}{n}}+C_{2}s^{\frac{n+1}{n}}+R(s)
with
C_{1},C_{2}>0
and

|R(s)|+s|R^{\prime}(s)|+s^{2}|R^{\prime\prime}(s)|=O(s^{\frac{n+2}{n}}).

(4.20)

(4)

If
L_{\infty}u=0
and
|u(s)|\leqslant Cs^{-\delta}
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, then
u
is a scalar multiple of
\hat{u}
.

Proof.

The uniqueness of
\hat{u}
is clear by the maximum principle.

If
\hat{u}
exists, then (1) can be proved as follows: First note that
\hat{u}\geqslant 0
because otherwise there is a global interior minimum with
\hat{u}<0
and this contradicts the maximum principle. Next, we prove that
\hat{u}
is weakly decreasing. Otherwise there exist
s_{1}<s_{2}
in
(0,1)
with
\hat{u}(s_{1})<\hat{u}(s_{2})
. Both of these values are nonnegative by what we said before. By the intermediate value theorem, there is an
s_{2}^{\prime}\in(s_{2},1)
such that
\hat{u}(s_{1})=\hat{u}(s_{2}^{\prime})
, but then
\hat{u}
attains a strictly positive interior maximum on
[s_{1},s_{2}^{\prime}]
, contradicting the maximum principle. Thus,
\hat{u}
is weakly decreasing. Next, we prove that
\hat{u}
is strictly decreasing. If this is false, then
\hat{u}
must be constant on some interval
[s_{1},s_{2}]\subset(0,1)
with
s_{1}<s_{2}
. From the ODE,
\hat{u}=0
on
[s_{1},s_{2}]
. Hence
\hat{u}=0
on
(0,1)
by interior analyticity, which contradicts
\hat{u}(0)=1
.

To prove the existence of
\hat{u}
, we solve
L_{\infty}\hat{u}_{\varepsilon}=0
on
[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]
with
\hat{u}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon)=1
and
\hat{u}_{\varepsilon}(1-\varepsilon)=0
for any fixed
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. This is possible by the standard Dirichlet problem for nonsingular ODEs. The same argument as above shows that
\hat{u}_{\varepsilon}
is strictly decreasing, hence in particular bounded by
0
and
1
. Then, also by standard ODE theory,
\hat{u}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies uniform derivative bounds to all orders on every fixed compact interval contained in
(0,1)
. Thus, up to subsequences,
\hat{u}_{\varepsilon}\to\hat{u}
locally smoothly on
(0,1)
, where
L_{\infty}\hat{u}=0
. The issue is to prove that
\hat{u}
is not identically zero, and, indeed, that it satisfies the correct boundary conditions as
s\to 0
and
s\to 1
. This will be done using a barrier argument.

First consider
\hat{u}_{sub}(s):=1-C_{1}s^{1/n}+C_{2}s^{\gamma}
for
C_{1},C_{2}>0
and
\gamma>1/n
to be determined. Fix an
s_{*}\in(0,1)
such that the
O(s^{(n+1)/n})
in (
4.7
) is explicitly bounded by
C_{*}s^{(n+1)/n}
for all
s\in(0,s_{*}]
. Here
s_{*},C_{*}
can be chosen to depend only on
n
. By choosing
C_{1},C_{2}
such that
C_{1}s_{*}{{}^{1/n}}=2
and
C_{2}s_{*}{{}^{\gamma}}=1
, we get
\hat{u}_{sub}(s_{*})=0
. It remains to check that
\hat{u}_{sub}
is a subsolution on
(0,s_{*}]
because then the maximum principle shows that
\hat{u}_{\varepsilon}\geqslant\hat{u}_{sub}
on
[\varepsilon,s_{*}]
, and so
\hat{u}\geqslant\hat{u}_{sub}
, proving that
\hat{u}
is not identically zero and in fact
\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\hat{u}(s)=1
. To this end we calculate on
(0,s_{*}]
, using (
4.7
) and the observation that
s^{1/n}
is a homogeneous solution of the operator
L_{\infty}+{\rm Id}
:

L_{\infty}\hat{u}_{sub}\geqslant(-1+C_{1}s^{\frac{1}{n}}-C_{2}s^{\gamma})+(0-C_{*}^{\prime}C_{1}s^{\frac{1}{n}})+(C_{*}^{\prime\prime}C_{2}(n\gamma-1)s^{\gamma-\frac{n+1}{n}}-C_{*}^{\prime\prime\prime}C_{2}s^{\gamma}).

(4.21)

Here,
C_{*}^{\prime}>0
depends only on
n
while
C_{*}^{\prime\prime},C_{*}^{\prime\prime\prime}>0
depend on
n
and
\gamma
. Thus, as long as
\frac{1}{n}<\gamma\leqslant\frac{n+1}{n}
, we can arrange that (
4.21
)
\geqslant 0
on
(0,s_{*}]
by making
s_{*}
smaller if necessary. To be precise, we first let
\gamma=\frac{n+1}{n}
and then choose
s_{*}
so small that
C_{2}=1/s_{*}^{\gamma}
satisfies
C_{*}^{\prime\prime}C_{2}(n\gamma-1)\geqslant 2+2C_{*}^{\prime}+C_{*}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

Next, we need to prove that
\hat{u}(s)\to 0
as
s\to 1^{-}
. In fact, by applying another barrier argument to the approximating functions
\hat{u}_{\varepsilon}
, we will prove that (2) holds. For this we again choose a
C_{*}>0
and an
\eta_{*}\in(0,1)
depending only on
n
such that the
O(\eta^{n+1})
in (
4.6
) is bounded by
C_{*}\eta^{n+1}
for all
\eta\in(0,\eta_{*}]
. Then we consider the function
\hat{u}^{sup}(1-\eta):=C_{1}\eta^{n+1}-C_{2}\eta^{\gamma}
for
C_{1},C_{2}>0
and
\gamma>n+1
to be determined. We set
C_{1}\eta_{*}^{n+1}=2
and
C_{2}\eta_{*}^{\gamma}=1
, thus arranging that
\hat{u}^{sup}(1-\eta_{*})=1
. To apply the maximum principle on
[1-\eta_{*},1)
, we need to check that
\hat{u}^{sup}
is a supersolution for
\eta\in(0,\eta_{*}]
after making
\eta_{*}
smaller if necessary. For this we compute for
\eta\in(0,\eta_{*}]
using (
4.6
):

L_{\infty}\hat{u}^{sup}\leqslant(-C_{1}\eta^{n+1}+C_{2}\eta^{\gamma})+(C_{1}\eta^{n+1}+C_{*}^{\prime}C_{1}\eta^{2n+2})+\left(-\frac{\gamma(\gamma-n)}{n+1}C_{2}\eta^{\gamma}+C_{*}^{\prime\prime\prime}C_{2}\eta^{\gamma+n+1}\right),
